On quantitative Laplace-type convergence results for some exponential probability measures, with two applications
Valentin De Bortoli, Agnès Desolneux

Abstract
Laplace-type results characterize the limit of sequence of measures ( π ε ) ε > 0 (\pi_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0} with density w.r.t the Lebesgue measure ( d π ε / d λ ) ( x ) ∝ exp [ − U ( x ) / ε ] (\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x)\propto\exp[-U(x)/\varepsilon] when the temperature ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 converges to 0 0 . If a limiting distribution π 0 \pi_{0} exists, it concentrates on the minimizers of the potential U U . Classical results require the invertibility of the Hessian of U U in order to establish such asymptotics. In this work, we study the particular case of norm-like potentials U U and establish quantitative bounds between π ε \pi_{\varepsilon} and π 0 \pi_{0} w.r.t. the Wasserstein distance of order 1 1 under an invertibility condition of a generalized Jacobian. One key element of our proof is the use of geometric measure theory tools such as the coarea formula. We apply our results to the study of maximum entropy models (microcanonical/macrocanonical distributions) and to the convergence of the iterates of the Stochastic Gradient Langevin Dynamics (SGLD) algorithm at low temperatures for non-convex minimization.



1
Introduction

Asymptotic expansions of integrals are ubiquitous in probability theory with
applications in simulated annealing

[
34
,
58
]
, Bayesian inference

[
41
,
65
,
63
]
,
statistical physics
[
25
]
or chaos expansion

[
48
]
. In this paper, we are interested in families of
Gibbs probability measures
\pi_{\varepsilon}
given by the density w.r.t the Lebesgue
measure

(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x)\propto\exp[-\|F(x)\|^{k}/\varepsilon]
,
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
F:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
is
some function with
F(0)=0
. In particular, we derive non asymptotic bounds
between
(\pi_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}
and
\pi_{0}
when
\varepsilon\to 0
.

Taking a step back, one way to deal with expansion of integrals of the form

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-U(x)/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x
where
U:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is
to rely on Laplace-type techniques, see

[
6
,
57
,
22
,
28
,
26
,
30
,
5
,
70
]

for instance. Assuming that
U
admits a unique minimizer
U(0)=0
and under
additional regularity conditions, it can be shown that if the Hessian of
U

evaluated at
0
is invertible then

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-U(x)/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\sim C_{U}\varepsilon^{d/2}
, with
explicit constant
C_{U}>0
(series expansions are also available under similar
conditions). This result on asymptotic integrals can be immediately used to
prove the convergence of probability measures with density w.r.t. the Lebesgue
measure
(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x)\propto\exp[-U(x)/\varepsilon]
, see

[
44
]
for instance. However the invertibility of the Hessian is a
restricting condition which is not satisfied in the setting of this paper where

U(x)=\|F(x)\|^{k}
in the case
p\leq d
.

When the Hessian
\nabla^{2}U(0)
is not invertible, the asymptotic integral expansion
becomes
degenerate
and is more difficult to analyze. However, several
approaches have been proposed in order to tackle this issue. For instance in

[
64
]
a multidimensional version of the Watson lemma
is established under restrictive conditions on the potential
U
near its
singularities. Another approach consists in integrating over the manifold of
minimizers and apply local change of variables
[
48
]
,
see also
[
4
,
11
]
. In particular,

[
48
]
derives an asymptotic integral expansion similar
to the one obtained in the non-degenerate case, under some invertibility
condition of the minors of the Hessian. Similarly,
[
44
]
uses the
tubular neighborhood theorem to obtain that
\pi_{\varepsilon}
converges to a
limiting measure under the assumption that the minimizers of
U
can be
partitioned into a collection of manifolds. Closer to the method introduced in
the present paper,
[
18
,
3
]
propose
to use the so-called
Gelfand-Leray forms
to tackle the non-degeneracy
problem. By integrating over the level sets it can be shown that

\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{V}}\varphi(x)\exp[-U(x)/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\exp[-t/\varepsilon](\int_{U^{-1}(t)\cap\mathsf{V}}\varphi(x)\omega_{U}(x))\mathrm{d}t\;,}

(2)

where
\mathsf{V}
is an open set,
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
a test
function and
\omega_{U}
the so-called Gelfand-Leray form associated with

U
. Then by establishing regularity properties for the functional

t\mapsto\int_{U^{-1}(t)}\varphi(x)\omega_{U}(x)
one can recover asymptotic
integral expansion in the case where
U
is analytic, see

[
18
,
3
]
. This theory does not rely
on any invertibility condition on the Hessian of
U
but the exponents appearing
in the asymptotic expansion are usually not available in closed form in general
since their derivation relies on resolution of singularities

[
42
]
.

In this paper, we consider a different approach which also does not rely on
invertibility conditions on the Hessian of
U
at singularity points but allows us
to derive quantitative expansions. To do so, we restrict the set of functions

U:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
to the set of norm-like functions,
i.e.

U
is
norm-like if there exists
F:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
U(x)=\|F(x)\|^{k}
(however our results can be
extended to general potentials
U
under classical invertibility conditions on
the Hessian of
U
). Instead of invertibility conditions of the Hessian of
U

we require invertibility conditions on the generalized Jacobian of
F
to be
defined below. Our approach relies on tools from the geometric measure theory
and in particular the coarea formula, see
[
1
]
for
instance. Indeed, using this formula we are able to establish a similar result
as (
2
) where the Gelfand-Leray form is replaced by a
twisted Hausdorff measure
\nu
. By establishing the Lipschitz regularity of the
mapping
t\mapsto\int_{U^{-1}(t)\cap\mathsf{V}}\varphi(x)\mathrm{d}\nu(x)
we are able to
provide quantitative bounds for the integral expansion. This expansion is the
key to our main result which establishes quantitative bounds w.r.t. the
Wasserstein distance of order
1
between
\pi_{\varepsilon}
and a limit probability
measure
\pi_{0}
for
\varepsilon>0
small enough. Concurrently to this work,

[
10
]
establishes similar quantitative results in the case
where the Hessian of
U
is invertible.

One of our main motivation for this study is the application of Laplace-type
results to maximum entropy models. In particular, using our quantitative bounds,
we are able to provide a link between two possible maximum entropy models
commonly used in statistical physics and image processing

[
12
,
21
]
. Given a reference measure
\mu
and
constraints
F:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
it is possible to define the

microcanonical model
, which corresponds to the probability measure

\pi_{\mathrm{mic}}
with the minimum Kullback-Leibler divergence w.r.t
\mu

and such that
\pi_{\mathrm{mic}}
is supported on the set

\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\;F(x)=0\}
. Similarly, one can define the

macrocanonical model
with level
\varepsilon>0
denoted

\pi_{\mathrm{mac}}^{\varepsilon}
such that
\pi_{\mathrm{mac}}^{\varepsilon}
minimizes
the Kullback-Leibler divergence w.r.t
\mu
and satisfies the integrability
condition

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|F(x)\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\pi_{\mathrm{mac}}^{\varepsilon}(x)=\varepsilon
. Using results from information geometry
[
19
]

and under mild regularity conditions,
\pi_{\varepsilon}
can be written as a Gibbs
measure and its limiting behavior when
\varepsilon\to 0
can be investigated in
the context of asymptotic expansions of integrals. In this work, we show that
the limit of the macrocanonical models when
\varepsilon\to 0
is a twisted
microcanonical model and propose an algorithm to asymptotically recover the
original microcanonical distribution.

Another application we consider in this paper is the non-asymptotic study of
Stochastic Gradient Langevin Dynamics (SGLD)
[
68
]
, a
popular algorithm used to approximate the minimizers of non-convex functions in a
machine learning setting. Given a potential (or population risk)

U:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
which can be written for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
as

U(x)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}u(x,z)\mathrm{d}\mu(z)
, we assume that we have access to an
empirical version of this risk for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

z^{1:n}=\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
given by

U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})=(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}u(x,z_{i})
as well as an unbiased estimator
of its gradient denoted
g
. Conditionally to the samples
z^{1:n}
, SGLD
corresponds to the recursion associated with an unadjusted Langevin dynamics

[
61
,
24
,
20
]

with target
U_{n}(\cdot,z^{1:n})/\varepsilon
for a small value of
\varepsilon>0
and
where the gradient of
U_{n}(\cdot,z^{1:n})
is replaced by its unbiased
estimator
g
. Since the invariant measure of the underlying Langevin diffusion
is given by
\pi_{\varepsilon}
with density w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure

(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x)\propto\exp[-U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})/\varepsilon]
we
obtain that for small values of the parameter
\varepsilon>0
, the samples

(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are approximately concentrated around the minimizers of

U_{n}(\cdot,z^{1:n})
. In existing works on SGLD

[
60
,
14
,
32
,
27
,
55
,
71
]

all quantitative theoretical guarantees are obtained w.r.t. the sequence of

(\mathbb{E}[U(X_{k})])_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. On the contrary, we characterize the
limiting distribution of SGLD and establish non-asymptotic quantitative bounds
between SGLD and this limit. Finally, we show that the support of the limiting
distribution is included into (but not necessarily equal to) the set of
minimizers of
U
when
n
is large. This last result relies on the notion of

thermodynamic barrier
which we introduce in the context of integral
expansion. To our
knowledge this is the first time that the importance of such a barrier to derive
parametric Laplace-type results is highlighted (as opposed to the
kinetic
barrier
which is a well-known quantity in simulated annealing

[
40
]
).

To summarize, our main contributions are three-fold:

(i)

We establish quantitative bounds between
\pi_{\varepsilon}
and
\pi_{0}
under
the assumption that the potential
U
is norm-like and additional regularity
conditions. In particular we provide an upper-bound between
\pi_{\varepsilon}
and

\pi_{0}
w.r.t. the Wasserstein distance of order
1
. We emphasize that our
results also hold under the more classical invertibility condition on the
Hessian of
U
.

(ii)

We apply this result to show that under mild conditions the limit of a
natural class of macrocanonical models is
not
the original
microcanonical model. However, we prove that a twisted sequence of
macrocanonical models converges to the microcanonical distribution. This
observation allows us to construct a Langevin-based algorithm in order to
sample from this distribution. We illustrate our method in low-dimensional
settings.

(iii)

Finally, we apply our theory to study the behavior of the iterates of SGLD
in the context of non-convex optimization. In particular, we characterize the
limiting distribution of SGLD at low temperature and show that it concentrates
on the minimizers of the population loss for a large number of samples using
the notion of
thermodynamic barrier
. Note that in this study, we no longer assume that

U
is norm-like but instead rely on invertibility conditions on the Hessian
of
U
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In
Section

2
we
present our main results,
i.e.
quantitative bounds between
\pi_{\varepsilon}
and
the limiting distribution
\pi_{0}
. In
Section

3
, we present our
two main applications: the links between the macrocanonical and microcanonical
maximum entropy models in
Section

3.1
, and a study of the
convergence of SGLD for non-convex minimization in

Section

3.2
. The proofs of our results are gathered in

Section

4
.

Notation

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
. We denote
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
the canonical basis of

\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
be the Euclidean scalar product
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\|\cdot\|
be the corresponding norm. We denote

\operatorname{B}(x,r)
the ball with center
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and radius
r>0
w.r.t. the
norm
\|\cdot\|
. Similarly, we denote
\operatorname{B}_{\infty}(x,r)
the ball with
center
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and radius
r>0
w.r.t the norm

\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
given for any
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\|x\|_{\infty}=\sup_{i\in\{1,\dots,d\}}\left|x_{i}\right|
.

Let
\mathsf{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote

\operatorname{diam}(\mathsf{A})=\sup_{x,y\in\mathsf{A}}\|x-y\|
its diameter and

\mathsf{A}^{\mathrm{c}}
its complementary set. Let
\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denote the
Borel
\sigma
-field of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
\mathsf{U}
be an open set of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

n\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
and let
\mathrm{C}^{n}(\mathsf{U},\mathbb{R}^{p})
be the set of the

n
-differentiable
\mathbb{R}^{p}
-valued functions defined over
\mathsf{U}
. If
p=1
we
simply denote
\mathrm{C}^{n}(\mathsf{U})
. Let
f\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathsf{U})
, we denote by
\nabla f
its
gradient. Furthermore, if
f\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathsf{U})
we denote
\nabla^{2}f
its Hessian
and
\Delta f
its Laplacian. By convention we denote
\nabla^{0}f=f
. We also
denote
\mathrm{C}(\mathsf{U},\mathbb{R}^{p})
the set of continuous functions defined over
\mathsf{U}
.
Let
f:\mathsf{A}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
p\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
and
\mathsf{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The
function
f
is said to be
\mathtt{L}
-Lipschitz with
\mathtt{L}\geq 0
if for any

x,y\in\mathsf{A}
,
\left\|f(x)-f(y)\right\|\leq\mathtt{L}\left\|x-y\right\|
. Let

F:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
F
is differentiable at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

and denote

\mathrm{D}F(x)=(\partial_{j}F_{i}(x))_{i\in\{1,\dots,p\},j\in\{1,\dots,d\}}

the Jacobian matrix of
F
which is a
p\times d
matrix. We define the
generalized Jacobian
\mathrm{J}F:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
given for any

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\mathrm{J}F(x)=\left\{\begin{matrix}\textstyle{\det(\mathrm{D}F(x)\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top})^{1/2}\;,\qquad\text{if $d\geq p$,}}\\
\textstyle{\det(\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top}\mathrm{D}F(x))^{1/2}\;,\qquad\text{if $d\leq p$.}}\end{matrix}\right.

(3)

Finally, if
F\in\mathrm{C}^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
with
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
, we define

\mathrm{D}^{j}F
for any
j\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
, recursively by

\mathrm{D}^{j+1}F=\mathrm{D}(\mathrm{D}^{j}F)
. Note that for any
j\in\{0,\dots,\ell\}
,

\mathrm{D}^{j}F
can be represented as
p\times d\times\dots\times d
tensor
(where
d
appears
j
times) with symmetric last
j
coordinates. Hence, we can
define
\mathrm{D}^{j+1}F^{\top}
the tensor where the first and last dimension have
been exchanged, which is a
d\times\dots d\times p
tensor. If
p=1
then,
we write
\nabla^{j}F=\mathrm{D}^{j}F
for any
j\in\{1,\dots,\ell\}
.

Let
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
be a measurable space. We denote by
\mathbb{F}(\mathsf{X})
the set
of the
\mathcal{X}/\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
-measurable real functions over
\mathsf{X}
. Let

\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{X})
be the set of finite signed measures over
\mathcal{X}
and let

\mu\in\mathbb{M}(\mathcal{X})
. For
f\in\mathbb{F}(\mathsf{X})
a
\mu
-integrable function
we denote

\textstyle{\mu[f]=\int_{\mathsf{X}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\;,}

(4)

the integral of
f
w.r.t. to
\mu
when it is
well-defined. We also define
\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
the set
of probability measures over
\mathcal{X}
and when there is no ambiguity on the
sigma-field we simply denote it by
\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{X})
. We denote by
\lambda
the Lebesgue
measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
and
(\mathsf{Y},\mathcal{Y})
be two measurable
spaces. A Markov kernel
\mathrm{K}
is a mapping

\mathrm{K}:\ \mathsf{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\left[0,1\right]
such that for any
x\in\mathsf{X}
,

\mathrm{K}(x,\cdot)\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{Y},\mathcal{Y})
and for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{Y}
,

\mathrm{K}(\cdot,\mathsf{A})
is measurable.

Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X})
. A probability measure

\zeta\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{X},\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{X})
is said to be a
transference plan between
\mu
and
\nu
if for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\zeta(\mathsf{A}\times\mathcal{X})=\mu(\mathsf{A})
and
\zeta(\mathcal{X}\times\mathsf{A})=\nu(\mathsf{A})
. We denote by
\mathbf{T}(\mu,\nu)
the set of all transference
plans between
\mu
and
\nu
. We define the Wasserstein metric/distance of order
\ell\geq 1

\mathbf{W}_{\ell}(\mu,\nu)
between
\mu
and
\nu
by

\textstyle{\mathbf{W}_{\ell}(\mu,\nu)^{\ell}=\inf_{\zeta\in\mathbf{T}(\mu,\nu)}\int_{\mathsf{X}^{2}}\|x-y\|^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\zeta(x,y)\;.}

(5)

We denote
\mathcal{H}^{r}
the Hausdorff measure of order
r>0
, given for any

\mathsf{A}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\mathcal{H}^{r}(\mathsf{A})=\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathcal{H}^{r,\delta}(\mathsf{A})
, where for any

\delta>0
we have

\textstyle{\mathcal{H}^{r,\delta}(\mathsf{A})=\inf\{\sum_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{diam}(\mathsf{U}_{i})^{r}\,:\;\mathsf{A}\subset\cup_{i\in\mathbb{N}}\mathsf{U}_{i}\;,\ \mathrm{diam}(\mathsf{U}_{i})\leq\delta\}\;.}

(6)

Basic facts on the Hausdorff measure are gathered in

Appendix

C
. Finally, we denote by
\mathtt{Poly}(k,\mathsf{A})
the set of
polynomials of
k\in\mathbb{N}
variables with coefficients in

\mathsf{A}\subset\mathbb{R}
.

2
Limit theorems

In this section, we state our main theorem and draw links with previous
approximation results for probability integrals. Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\bar{\varepsilon}>0
,
F:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
d,p\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
such that for
any
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon})
,

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\left\|F(x)\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x<+\infty
. For any

\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon})
, we define

\pi_{\varepsilon}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\textstyle{\pi_{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{A})=\left.\int_{\mathsf{A}}\exp[-\left\|F(x)\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\middle/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\left\|F(x)\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\right.\;.}

(7)

Our goal is to study the behavior of
\pi_{\varepsilon}
when
\varepsilon\to 0
. In
particular, we identify a limit
\pi_{0}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and derive
non-asymptotic convergence bounds. For any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we let
(when it is well-defined)

\textstyle{\pi_{0}(\mathsf{A})=\left.\int_{F^{-1}(0)\cap\mathsf{A}}\mathrm{J}F(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-\min(d,p)}(x)\middle/\int_{F^{-1}(0)}\mathrm{J}F(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-\min(d,p)}(x)\right.\;.}

(8)

In what follows we consider the following assumption on
F
which implies that

\pi_{\varepsilon}
is well-defined for any
\varepsilon\geq 0
.

H 1
.

F\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
and
F(0)=0
. For any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
,

\mathrm{J}F(x)\neq 0
. There exist
\mathtt{m},\upalpha>0
and
R\geq 0
such that for any

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\|x\right\|\geq R
,
\left\|F(x)\right\|\geq\mathtt{m}\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha}
.

A few remarks are in order. First, the assumption
F(0)=0
is only technical and
can be replaced by
F^{-1}(0)\neq\emptyset
. Similarly, the smoothness
assumption
F\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
allows us to avoid some
technicalities in the proofs but can be relaxed. Second, we assume that

x\mapsto\|F(x)\|
grows at least polynomially when

\left\|x\right\|\to+\infty
. This condition can also be relaxed to handle
sub-polynomial growth at infinity. However, changing the rate of growth might
affect the quantitative convergence properties of
(\pi_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}

towards
\pi_{0}
. A study of sub-polynomial growth is left for future
work. Finally the assumption that for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
,
\mathrm{J}F(x)\neq 0
is
necessary in order to define
\pi_{0}
.

Theorem 1
.

Assume
1
. Then for any
\varepsilon\geq 0
,

\pi_{\varepsilon}
is well-defined. Let
\mathsf{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
open and bounded
such that
F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{U}
. Let
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and

C_{\varphi}\geq 0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{\left|\varphi(x)\right|\leq C_{\varphi}\exp[C_{\varphi}\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha k}]\;,}

(9)

with
\upalpha>0
defined in
1
and
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
in
(
7
). Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left|\pi_{\varepsilon}[\varphi]-\pi_{0}[\varphi]\right|=0
. In
addition, if
\varphi
is
M_{1,\varphi}
-Lipschitz on
\mathsf{U}
with

M_{1,\varphi}\geq 0
, then there exist

A\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+}),\bar{\varepsilon}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}(C_{\varphi}))
we have

\left|\pi_{\varepsilon}[\varphi]-\pi_{0}[\varphi]\right|\leq A(C_{\varphi})(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})\varepsilon^{1/k}\;,

(10)

with

M_{0,\varphi}=\sup\{|\varphi(x)|\,:\;x\in\mathsf{U}\}
,

A,\bar{\varepsilon}
independent functions from
\varphi
,
A
is non-decreasing and

\bar{\varepsilon}
is non-increasing.

Proof.

We provide a sketch of the proof. The whole proof is postponed to

Section

4.1
. We first start by showing that we can restrict our
study to versions of
\pi_{\varepsilon}
and
\pi_{0}
with truncated support. Then
our study differs depending on if
d\geq p
or
d\leq p
. If
d\geq p
then
we use tools from geometric measure theory, and in particular the coarea
formula in combination with the Lipschitz property of applications of the form

t\mapsto\int_{F^{-1}(t)}\varphi(x)\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-p}(x)
. In the case

d\leq p
we adapt arguments from Morse theory and Laplace theory to derive
quantitative bounds on
\left|\pi_{\varepsilon}[\varphi]-\pi_{0}[\varphi]\right|
. These
analyses are conducted with smooth test functions

\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and we relax this hypothesis using
smoothing arguments.
∎

We highlight a few key points from this theorem and draw links with the existing
literature.

(a)

Our result is related to the Laplace-type convergence results of

[
70
,
48
,
4
,
11
,
11
,
6
,
57
,
22
,
28
,
26
,
30
,
5
]
. In these works, the authors study integrals of the form

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\exp[-U(x)/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x
in the limiting case
where
\varepsilon\to 0
under regularity assumptions on
U
and
\varphi
, and
the non-degeneracy condition that for any
x\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}U
,

\nabla^{2}U(x)
is invertible. We say that
U:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is
norm-like with exponent
2
if there exist
p\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
and

F:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that
U(x)=\left\|F(x)\right\|^{2}
. In this case, and
under additional regularity assumptions, we have for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\nabla^{2}U(x)=2\mathrm{D}^{2}F(x)^{\top}F(x)+2\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top}\mathrm{D}F(x)\;.

(11)

In particular for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
,
\nabla^{2}U(x)
is invertible if and
only if
\mathrm{J}F(x)\neq 0
which is precisely the non-degeneracy condition
imposed in
1
(note that this directly implies that
d\leq p
). For any
U\in\mathrm{C}^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}_{+})
with Lipschitz third order
derivatives, there exist
p\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
and
F\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})

with Lipschitz derivatives such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

U(x)=\left\|F(x)\right\|^{2}
, see

[
7
,
31
]
. Hence, our restriction to
functions
U:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
of the form
U=\left\|F\right\|^{k}
for some

F:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
p,k\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
is not too constraining under
additional regularity assumptions.

(b)

We emphasize that our results can be
extended to a general potential
U\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}_{+})
if the
Hessian of
U
is invertible on the set
U^{-1}(0)
, thus recovering the usual
setting of Laplace-type convergence results.

(c)

As highlighted previously, we aim at providing quantitative bounds for

\left|\pi_{\varepsilon}[\varphi]-\pi_{0}[\varphi]\right|
. In the case where
d\leq p

we adapt classical arguments from Morse and Laplace theory. Our main
contribution is the use of geometric measure theory to cover the case

d\geq p
which cannot be treated with classical Laplace arguments. The case

d\geq p
is of particular interest in machine learning applications
requiring to sample from distributions of the form

(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x)\propto\exp[-\left\|F(x)\right\|^{2}]
, where

F:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
is an encoder neural network with
d\geq p
. In

Section

3.1
we investigate this situation in details in the case
of generative modeling with macrocanonical and microcanonical models.

As a by-product of
Theorem

1
we obtain the following corollary which
establishes quantitative bounds for the Wasserstein distance of order
1

between
\pi_{\varepsilon}
and
\pi_{0}
.

Corollary 2
.

Assume
1
. Then for any
\varepsilon\geq 0
,

\pi_{\varepsilon}
is well-defined. In addition, there exist
A_{1}\geq 0
and

\bar{\varepsilon}>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in\left[0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right)
we have

\mathbf{W}_{1}(\pi_{\varepsilon},\pi_{0})\leq A_{1}\varepsilon^{1/k}\;,

(12)

where we recall that
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
is defined in (
7
).

Proof.

Denote

\mathrm{Lip}=\{\varphi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\,:\;\left|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\right|\leq\left\|x-y\right\|\;,\ \text{for any }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
. In addition, denote

\mathrm{Lip}_{0}=\{\varphi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}\,:\;\left|\varphi(x)-\varphi(y)\right|\leq\left\|x-y\right\|\;,\ \text{for any }x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\ \varphi(0)=0\}
. First, using
[
66
, Theorem 5.10]
we
have for any
\varepsilon>0
.

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{1}(\pi_{\varepsilon},\pi_{0})=\sup\{\pi_{\varepsilon}[\varphi]-\pi_{0}[\varphi]\,:\;\varphi\in\mathrm{Lip}\}=\sup\{\left|\pi_{\varepsilon}[\varphi]-\pi_{0}[\varphi]\right|\,:\;\varphi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}\}\;.

(13)

Let
r=\upalpha k
and
\beta_{r}=\left\lceil 1/r\right\rceil
. For any

\varphi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\left\|x\right\|\geq 1
we have

\beta_{r}\exp[\beta_{r}\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha k}]\geq\beta_{r}^{\beta_{r}}/(\beta_{r}!)\left\|x\right\|^{r\beta_{r}}\geq\left\|x\right\|\geq\left|\varphi(x)\right|\;.

(14)

Similarly, if
\left\|x\right\|\leq 1
we have that

\left|\varphi(x)\right|\leq 1\leq\beta_{r}\exp[\beta_{r}\left\|x\right\|]
. Hence for any

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\left|\varphi(x)\right|\leq\beta_{r}\exp[\beta_{r}\left\|x\right\|]
. For
any
\varphi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}
we have
M_{1,\varphi}=1
. In addition, the set

F^{-1}(0)
is compact since

\lim_{\left\|x\right\|\to+\infty}\left\|F(x)\right\|=+\infty
and

F\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
. Therefore there exists
R>0
such that

F^{-1}(0)\subset\operatorname{B}(0,R)
. In particular, note that
\mathsf{U}=\operatorname{B}(0,R)

is open and bounded. We have that for any
\varphi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}
and

x\in\mathsf{U}

\left|\varphi(x)\right|\leq\left\|x\right\|\leq R\;.

(15)

Therefore we get that
M_{0,\varphi}=R
. Combining these results with

Theorem

1
we have for any
\varphi\in\mathrm{Lip}_{0}
and

\varepsilon\in\left[0,\bar{\varepsilon}(\left\lceil 1/r\right\rceil)\right)

\left|\pi_{\varepsilon}[\varphi]-\pi_{0}[\varphi]\right|\leq A(C_{\varphi})(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})\varepsilon^{1/k}\leq 2A(\left\lceil 1/r\right\rceil)(1+R)\varepsilon^{1/k}\;.

(16)

We conclude the proof upon combining this result and (
13
).
∎

Concurrently to our work,
[
10
]
establishes similar
quantitative bounds w.r.t the Wasserstein distance of order
1
without the
norm-like assumption on the potential
U
but assuming that the Hessian of
U

is invertible on
\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{U(x)\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
, see
[
10
, Lemma
4.6]
. To do so, the authors derive estimates of the form

\mathbf{W}_{1}(\pi_{\varepsilon_{1}},\pi_{\varepsilon_{2}})\leq C|\varepsilon_{2}^{1/2}-\varepsilon_{1}^{1/2}|
with
C\geq 0
for

\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}>0
using a coupling lemma and results from Morse
theory. The final bound is obtained by letting
\varepsilon_{2}\to 0
in the previous
inequality and the fact that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\pi_{\varepsilon}=\pi_{0}

weakly.
Section

2
extends these bounds to the case where the Hessian
of
U
is no longer invertible under a norm-like condition on the potential.

Also, note that
[
44
]
establishes that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\pi_{\varepsilon}=\pi_{0}
weakly without any norm-like
assumption on
U
under the condition that

\mathsf{C}^{\star}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{U(x)\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
can be partitioned
into a collection of manifolds and that for any
x\in\mathsf{C}^{\star}
, the Hessian
of
U
along the normal plan to the manifold associated with
x
is invertible
(we denote
\nabla_{\mathrm{N}}^{2}U(x)
this quantity). In

Lemma

A.1
, we show that if
U=\|F\|^{2}
then we have
that
\det(\nabla_{\mathrm{N}}^{2}U(x))=\mathrm{J}F(x)^{2}
for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
. Hence, the invertibility condition can be written as: for any

x\in F^{-1}(0)
,
\mathrm{J}F(x)\neq 0
similarly to
1
and the limiting
measure identified by
[
44
]
is exactly
\pi_{0}
.

The next result is an extension of
Theorem

1
where, given

k\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
,
\Psi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
\pi_{\varepsilon}
is replaced by

\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}
defined for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by

\textstyle{\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}(\mathsf{A})=\left.\int_{\mathsf{A}}\Psi(x)\exp[-\left\|F(x)\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\middle/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Psi(x)\exp[-\left\|F(x)\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\right.\;.}

(17)

Similarly, for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we let
(when it is well-defined)

\textstyle{\pi_{0}^{\Psi}(\mathsf{A})=\left.\int_{F^{-1}(0)\cap\mathsf{A}}\Psi(x)\mathrm{J}F(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-\min(d,p)}(x)\middle/\int_{F^{-1}(0)}\Psi(x)\mathrm{J}F(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-\min(d,p)}(x)\right.\;.}

(18)

We consider the following assumption on
\Psi
which ensures that

\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}
is well-defined for
\varepsilon\geq 0
when combined with

1
.

H 2
.

\Psi\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}_{+})
, there exists
C_{\Psi}
such that for any

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\Psi(x)\leq C_{\Psi}\exp[C_{\Psi}\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha k}]
and

\Psi(x)>0
for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
.

Note that in
2
,
\upalpha>0
is given in
1
and

k\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
is given in (
7
) Under this assumption, we
derive the following quantitative bounds.

Theorem 3
.

Assume
1
and

2
. Then for any
\varepsilon\geq 0
,
\pi_{\varepsilon}

is well-defined. Let
\mathsf{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
open and bounded such that

F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{U}
. Let
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and

C_{\varphi}\geq 0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\left|\varphi(x)\right|\leq C_{\varphi}\exp[C_{\varphi}\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha k}]\;,

(19)

with
\upalpha>0
defined in
1
and
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
in
(
7
). Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left|\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}[\varphi]-\pi_{0}^{\Psi}[\varphi]\right|=0
. In addition, if
\varphi
and
\Psi
are respectively

M_{1,\varphi}
-Lipschitz and
M_{1,\Psi}
-Lipschitz on
\mathsf{U}
with

M_{1,\varphi},M_{1,\Psi}\geq 0
, then there exist

A\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
\bar{\varepsilon}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})

such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}(C_{\varphi}))
we have

\textstyle{|\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}[\varphi]-\pi_{0}^{\Psi}[\varphi]|\leq A(C_{\varphi})(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})\varepsilon^{1/k}\;,}

(20)

with

M_{0,\varphi}=\sup\{|\varphi(x)|\,:\;x\in\mathsf{U}\}
,

A,\bar{\varepsilon}
independent functions from
\varphi
,
A
non-decreasing and

\bar{\varepsilon}
non-increasing.

Proof.

The proof is postponed to

Section

4.1
.
∎

The same remarks formulated after
Theorem

1
hold for this
extension. In particular the norm-like assumption on
U
can be omitted and
replaced by a classical invertibility condition on the Hessian at
U^{-1}(0)
.
Note that
Theorem

1
is a direct consequence of

Theorem

3
upon letting
\Psi=1
. An important consequence
of
Theorem

3
is the case where for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\Psi(x)=\mathrm{J}F(x)
. Indeed, doing so we obtain that
\pi_{0}^{\Psi}
is the uniform
distribution on
F^{-1}(0)
,
i.e.
the maximum entropy distribution with support

F^{-1}(0)
. We discuss this special case in

Section

3.1
. Similarly to
Section

2
we can also
establish the following corollary.

Corollary 4
.

Assume
1
and

2
. Then for any
\varepsilon\geq 0
,
\pi_{\varepsilon}

is well-defined. In addition, assume that
\Psi
is Lipschitz
continuous. Then there exist
A_{2}\geq 0
and
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
such that for any

\varepsilon\in\left(0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right)
we have

\mathbf{W}_{1}(\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi},\pi_{0}^{\Psi})\leq A_{2}\varepsilon^{1/k}\;,

(21)

where we recall that
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
is defined in (
7
).

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of
Section

2
.
∎

3
Applications

In this section, we present two applications of our results. First, we draw links
between two different maximum entropy models in
Section

3.1
. Then,
we study the convergence of SGLD for non-convex minimization in

Section

3.2
.

3.1
Maximum entropy distribution

3.1.1
Maximum entropy models

Let
\mathsf{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a compact space with

\mathrm{int}(\mathsf{X})\neq\emptyset
and
G:\mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
measurable. The
maximum entropy model with constraint
G
is denoted
\pi_{\mathrm{Ent}}
and is
given by

\textstyle{\pi_{\mathrm{Ent}}=\operatorname*{arg\,max}\{H(\pi)\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{\lambda}(\mathsf{X}),\ \pi[\|G\|]<+\infty,\ \pi[G]=0\}}\;,

(22)

where
\mathscr{P}_{\lambda}(\mathsf{X})
is the set of probability measures on
\mathsf{X}
which
admit a density w.r.t the Lebesgue measure,

\pi[G]=\int_{\mathsf{X}}G(x)\mathrm{d}\pi(x)
and

H(\pi)=-\int_{\mathsf{X}}\log(\mathrm{d}\pi/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x)\mathrm{d}\pi(x)
for any

\pi\in\mathscr{P}_{\lambda}(\mathsf{X})
, where
\lambda
is the Lebesgue measure on

\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Note that
H(\pi)
is well-defined (but can be infinite) since
\mathsf{X}
is
compact and in
this case
\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\mu)=-H(\pi)+\log(\lambda(\mathsf{X}))
, where
\mu
is the
uniform measure on
\mathsf{X}
and we recall that
\lambda
is the Lebesgue measure.

Such maximum entropy models naturally arise in many areas such as
statistical physics
[
46
,
12
]
, econometrics

[
38
]
, generative modeling

[
59
,
52
,
21
]
, reinforcement
learning
[
76
]
or image processing

[
35
,
23
]
. In what follows, we introduce
two extensions of (
22
). More precisely, we use the analogy
between the maximization of the entropy and the minimization of an appropriate
Kullback-Leibler divergence to extend (
22
) on non-compact
spaces, following
[
21
]
.

Macrocanonical distributions

Given
\mu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

G:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
measurable, we say that

\pi^{\star}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a macrocanonical distribution

[
12
]
with reference measure
\mu
and constraint
G
if it
satisfies

\textstyle{\pi^{\star}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\mu)\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ \pi[\|G\|]<+\infty,\ \pi[G]=0\}}\;.

(23)

Using results from information geometry
[
19
]
any
macrocanonical model can be written as an exponential distribution under mild
assumptions.

Proposition 5

(
[
21
]
)
.

Assume that
G\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
and that there exist

\upalpha,\upbeta,\eta>0
with
\upbeta>\upalpha
such that

\textstyle{\sup\{\|G(x)\|(1+\|x\|^{\upalpha})^{-1}\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}<+\infty\;,\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[\eta\|x\|^{\upbeta}]\mathrm{d}\mu(x)<+\infty\;,}

(24)

In addition, assume that for any
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
with

\|\theta\|=1
, we have

\mu(\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\;\langle\theta,G(x)\rangle<0\})>0
.
Then there exists a unique macrocanonical distribution with constraint
G
and
reference measure
\mu
denoted
\pi^{\star}
. In addition, there exists

\theta^{\star}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\pi^{\star}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)=\exp[-\langle\theta^{\star},G(x)\rangle-L(\theta^{\star})]\;,\qquad L(\theta^{\star})=\log\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\langle\theta^{\star},G(x)\rangle]\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\;.}

(25)

We refer to
[
21
]
for a relaxation of these conditions and a
detailed study of the macrocanonical model.
Section

3.1.1
ensures
that macrocanonical distributions can be written as Gibbs measures. We emphasize
that these distributions satisfy a constraint in expectation. An alternative way
to define maximum entropy models under constraints is to ensure almost sure
equality. This tighter constraint yields new maximum entropy models called
microcanonical distributions.

Microcanonical distributions

A microcanonical
model
\pi^{\star}\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with reference measure
\mu
and constraint

G
can be defined as

\textstyle{\pi^{\star}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{\operatorname{KL}(\pi|\mu)\,:\;\pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\ \pi[\|G\|]=0\}}\;.

(26)

We emphasize that imposing (
26
) is equivalent to impose that

\pi^{\star}
has minimum Kullback-Leibler divergence w.r.t
\mu
among all
distributions which satisfy
G=0
almost surely. Note that if there exists a
microcanonical model
\pi^{\star}
with
\operatorname{KL}(\pi^{\star}|\mu)<+\infty
then

\mu(\mathsf{A})>0
where
\mathsf{A}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\;G(x)=0\}
and in
this case
\pi^{\star}=\mu(\cdot\cap\mathsf{A})/\mu(\mathsf{A})
. In what follows, we will
say that
\pi^{\star}
is the uniform microcanonical distribution with constraint

G
if
G^{-1}(0)
is compact with
\mathcal{H}^{d-\min(d,p)}(G^{-1}(0))<+\infty
and

\pi^{\star}
is the uniform distribution on
G^{-1}(0)
,
i.e.
for any

\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\textstyle{\pi^{\star}(\mathsf{A})=\int_{G^{-1}(0)\cap\mathsf{A}}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-\min(d,p)}(x)/\mathcal{H}^{d-\min(d,p)}(G^{-1}(0))\;.}

(27)

Note that contrary to macrocanonical distributions which admit a
representation as a Gibbs measure, see
Section

3.1.1
, the
microcanonical models are concentrated on the set
G^{-1}(0)
. In the next
section, we draw links between these two maximum entropy distributions.

3.1.2
From macrocanonical to microcanonical

In order to draw links between macrocanonical and microcanonical models we
consider a specific sequence of macrocanonical models associated with the
constraint
G_{\varepsilon}=\|F\|^{2}-\varepsilon
for
\varepsilon>0
. Let

\mu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a reference probability measure. Under the
conditions of
Section

3.1.1
, we define a family of measures

\{\rho_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
such that for any
\varepsilon>0
,
\rho_{\varepsilon}

is the macrocanonical distribution associated with
G_{\varepsilon}
and
\mu
. More
precisely, for any
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\theta_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}
such
that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\rho_{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\mu)(x)=\exp[-\theta_{\varepsilon}\|F(x)\|^{2}-L_{\varepsilon}]\;,\quad L_{\varepsilon}=\log\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\theta_{\varepsilon}\|F(x)\|^{2}]\mathrm{d}\mu(x)\;.}

(28)

Our goal is to study the behavior of the family
\{\rho_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}

when
\varepsilon\to 0
. In particular, we show that there exists a reference
measure
\mu
such that the limit
\pi_{0}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\rho_{\varepsilon}

exists where
\pi_{0}
is the uniform microcanonical distribution
(
27
). We start with the following proposition which ensures
that
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\theta_{\varepsilon}=+\infty
with linear rate.

Proposition 6
.

Let
\mu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
F:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
. Assume that the
conditions of
Section

3.1.1
with

G_{\varepsilon}=\|F\|^{2}-\varepsilon
for any
\varepsilon>0
are satisfied
and let
\rho_{\varepsilon}
be the macrocanonical distribution
with constraint
G_{\varepsilon}
and reference measure
\mu
. Assume that there
exists
\Psi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\mu
admits a density
w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure given by
\Psi
. In addition, assume that

1
and
2
hold. Then, we have that

\theta_{\varepsilon}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}p/(2\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

We provide a sketch of the proof. The whole proof is postponed to

Section

4.2
. In the case where
d\geq p
we use tools from
geometric measure theory to derive an equivalent of

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Psi(x)\|F(x)\|^{2}\exp[-\|F(x)\|^{2}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x

when
\varepsilon\to 0
akin to
Section

4.1.1
. In the case

d\leq p
, using Morse lemma and classical Laplace analysis we show a similar
result. We conclude upon combining these results and that

\rho_{\varepsilon}[\|F\|^{2}]=\varepsilon
.
∎

Note that the equivalent in
Section

3.1.2
is independent from the reference measure
\mu
.
In
Section

4.2
, we prove an extension of
Section

3.1.2
,
see
Section

4.2.3
. In particular we show that

G_{\varepsilon}=\|F\|^{2}-\varepsilon
can be replaced by

G_{\varepsilon}=\|F\|^{k}-\varepsilon
with
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
under similar
conditions. In particular, we get that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
,

\theta_{\varepsilon}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{C}_{k}/\varepsilon
with

\mathcal{C}_{k}
explicit if
k=2
. Finally note that under
the same conditions as
Section

3.1.2
, we have that

\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}[\|F\|^{2}]\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}p/(2\varepsilon)
, where we
recall that for any
\varepsilon>0
and
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\textstyle{\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}(\mathsf{A})=\int_{\mathsf{A}}\Psi(x)\exp[-\|F(x)\|^{2}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Psi(x)\exp[-\|F(x)\|^{2}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\;.}

(29)

This comes from the facts that
\rho_{\varepsilon}=\pi_{\theta_{\varepsilon}}^{\Psi}
and
\rho_{\varepsilon}[\|F\|^{2}]=\varepsilon
.

The following proposition establishes quantitative bounds w.r.t. the Wasserstein
of order
1
between
\{\rho_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
and a limiting measure. We
obtain this result upon combining
Section

3.1.2
and

Section

2
.

Proposition 7
.

Assume the same conditions as
Section

3.1.2
. Then there exist

\bar{\varepsilon}>0
and
A_{3}\geq 0
such that for any

\varepsilon\in\left(0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right]
we have

\mathbf{W}_{1}(\rho_{\varepsilon},\pi_{0}^{\Psi})\leq A_{3}\varepsilon^{1/2}\;,

(30)

where
\pi_{0}^{\Psi}
is given in (
18
).

Section

3.1.2
establishes quantitative bounds between the family
of macrocanonical models
\{\rho_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
and a limiting
distribution
\pi_{0}^{\Psi}
. Note that this limiting distribution is
not

the uniform microcanonical distribution
\pi^{\star}
defined in
(
27
) in general, even though it is supported on the set

F^{-1}(0)
. However, it is possible to sample from this
\pi^{\star}
by
choosing
\mu
such that for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
,

\Psi(x)=(\mathrm{d}\mu/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x)=\mathrm{J}F(x)
.

3.1.3
Some simple experiments

Methodology

In this experimental section we consider two simple examples of functions

F:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
with
d=1
or
2
and
p=1
. We consider the associated models

\{\pi_{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
and
\{\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}\}_{\varepsilon>0}

and their limit as
\varepsilon
goes to
0
, showing in particular that for

\Psi=\mathrm{J}F
, it converges to the uniform microcanonical model that is the
uniform distribution on
F^{-1}(0)
. We also check experimentally the scaling
relation of Proposition
3.1.2
.

Zeros of a polynomial

In this first example, let
P:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a polynomial. We are interested in the
zeros of
P
, and, more precisely in sampling in a uniform way on the set of the
zeros of
P
. Using the notations of the previous sections, we set
F=P
. For

\varepsilon>0
, let us define the two distributions, with respective densities with
respect to the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{R}
given for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x)=\exp[-F(x)^{2}/\varepsilon]/\int_{\mathbb{R}}\exp[-F(\tilde{x})^{2}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}}\;,

(31)

\displaystyle\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x)=\mathrm{J}F(x)\exp[-F(x)^{2}/\varepsilon]/\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{J}F(\tilde{x})\exp[-F(\tilde{x})^{2}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}}\;,

(32)

where for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\Psi(x)=\mathrm{J}F(x)=|P^{\prime}(x)|
.

In
Figure

1
, we display the polynomial
P
where for any

x\in\mathbb{R}
,
P(x)=x(x-0.5)(x-1.7)(x-2.5)
. We also check numerically the
scaling relation of Proposition
3.1.2
. Then, in

Figure

2
, we illustrate the different behaviors of
\pi_{\varepsilon}

and
\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}
when
\varepsilon
is small (
\varepsilon=10^{-3}
in our experiments). We
also present the histograms obtained using of
N=10^{4}
independent samples of

\pi_{\varepsilon}
and of
\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}
. The stars indicate the target limit
distributions (that is the uniform distribution on the zeros of
P
for the
limit of
\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}
).

Figure 1:
Left: graph of the polynomial
x\mapsto P(x)
, that has
4

zeros. Right: verifying the scaling relation of
Section

3.1.2
by
plotting
\varepsilon\mapsto 2\pi_{\varepsilon}[P^{2}]
, which is equivalent to
\varepsilon
as
\varepsilon

goes to
0
.

Figure 2:
Distributions
\pi_{\varepsilon}
and
\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}
(first line),
and histogram of their samples from them (second line). This experiment shows
that the limit distribution of
\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}
as
\varepsilon
goes to
0
is
the uniform microcanonical model given by the uniform distribution on the
zeros of
P
.

Two-dimensional ellipse

In this second example, we consider the function
F:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
given for any

x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
by
F(x)=a_{1}x_{1}^{2}+a_{2}x_{2}^{2}-1
with
a_{1},a_{2}>0
. For

\varepsilon>0
, we define
\pi_{\varepsilon}
and
\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}
whose densities w.r.t
the Lebesgue measure are given for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
by

\displaystyle\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varepsilon}/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x)=\exp[-\|F(x)\|^{2}/\varepsilon]/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\exp[-\|F(\tilde{x})\|^{2}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}}\;,

(33)

\displaystyle\textstyle{(\mathrm{d}\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}/\mathrm{d}\lambda)(x)=\mathrm{J}F(x)\exp[-\|F(x)\|^{2}/\varepsilon]/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{J}F(\tilde{x})\exp[-\|F(\tilde{x})\|^{2}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}}\;,

(34)

To sample from
\pi_{\varepsilon}
and
\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}
, we use two Markov chains given by
the Unadjusted Langevin Algorithm

[
61
,
24
,
20
]
,
given respectively by
X_{0},Y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and the recursions

\displaystyle X_{n+1}=X_{n}-\gamma(\nabla\|F\|^{2}(X_{n})/\varepsilon)+\sqrt{2\gamma}Z_{n+1}\;,

(35)

\displaystyle Y_{n+1}=Y_{n}-\gamma(\nabla\|F\|^{2}(Y_{n})/\varepsilon-\nabla\log\mathrm{J}F(Y_{n}))+\sqrt{2\gamma}Z_{n+1}\;,

(36)

where
\gamma>0
is a stepsize and
\{Z_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a family of
independent Gaussian random variables with zero mean and identity covariance
matrix. For any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
we have

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathrm{J}F(x)=2(a_{1}^{2}x_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}x_{2}^{2})^{1/2}\;,}\quad\textstyle{\nabla\|F\|^{2}(x)=2F(x)\nabla F(x)=4(a_{1}x_{1}^{2}+a_{2}x_{2}^{2}-1)\left(\begin{array}[]{l}a_{1}x_{1}\\
a_{2}x_{2}\end{array}\right)\;,}

\displaystyle\textstyle{\nabla\log\mathrm{J}F(x)=(a_{1}^{2}x_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}x_{2}^{2})^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{l}a_{1}^{2}x_{1}\\
a_{2}^{2}x_{2}\end{array}\right)}\;.

The set
F^{-1}(0)
is an ellipse, of cartesian equation

a_{1}x_{1}^{2}+a_{2}x_{2}^{2}=1
and has a polar parametrization given for any

\theta\in[0,2\uppi)
by

\textstyle{r(\theta)=(a_{2}+(a_{1}-a_{2})\cos^{2}\theta)^{1/2}\;.}

This is not an arc-length parametrization, and the infinitesimal length of the
curve element between
\theta
and
\theta+d\theta
is given by

\ell(\theta)d\theta
where for any
\theta\in\left(0,2\uppi\right]
we have

\ell(\theta)=(r(\theta)^{2}+r^{\prime}(\theta)^{2})^{1/2}=(a_{2}^{2}+(a_{1}^{2}-a_{2}^{2})\cos^{2}\theta)^{1/2}(a_{2}+(a_{1}-a_{2})\cos^{2}\theta)^{-3/2}\;.

In
Figure

3
, we set
a_{1}=1
,
a_{2}=4
,
\varepsilon=10^{-3}
,

\gamma=10^{-5}
,
N=9\times 10^{7}
iterations in the Markov chains, and
histograms with bins of size
0.05\uppi
. In
Figure

3
, we compare
two different histograms: the ones of the angle values (
\theta
) for the points
of the chain
X_{n}
(diamonds), of the chain
Y_{n}
(stars), and the two
distributions with density

\theta\mapsto\ell(\theta)/\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\ell(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta
(black
curve) and

\theta\mapsto\ell(\theta)\mathrm{J}F(\theta)^{-1}/\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\ell(\theta)\mathrm{J}F(\theta)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\theta
(red curve). Note that

\theta\mapsto\ell(\theta)/\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\ell(\theta)\mathrm{d}\theta

corresponds to the Hausdorff measure on the ellipse pushed by the polar
parametrisation
\theta\mapsto r(\theta)
,
i.e.
the uniform microcanonical
model pushed by
\theta\mapsto r(\theta)
. We observe that the Markov chain
corrected with the generalized Jacobian indeed achieves the uniform
microcanonical model on the set
F^{-1}(0)
. Finally, we also check
experimentally the scaling relation of Proposition
3.1.2
.

Figure 3:
Left: histogram of angles of the samples
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and

(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, showing that
\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}
is close to the uniform
distribution on the ellipse for small values of
\varepsilon>0
. Middle: the
trajectory of the first
10^{6}
samples of
Y_{n}
(with a burnin of
10^{3}
iterates). Right: checking the scaling
relation of Proposition
3.1.2
.

3.2
Non-convex minimization

3.2.1
Non-convex setting and related work

In this section, we consider the following minimization problem:

\text{find }x^{\star}\in\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{U(x)\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}\;,

(41)

with
U\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and

\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{U(x)\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}\neq\emptyset
. Here, we do not
assume that
U
is convex. Hence, classical first-order optimization schemes
such as gradient descent might get trapped in saddle points. Adding
isotropic Gaussian noise to this dynamics circumvents this issue, see

[
9
,
58
]
for instance. The algorithm is
then given by the following recursion:
X_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for any

k\in\mathbb{N}

X_{k+1}=X_{k}-\gamma_{k}\nabla U(X_{k})+Z_{k+1}\;,

(42)

where
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of independent Gaussian random
variables with zero mean and covariance matrix
\sigma_{k}^{2}\operatorname{Id}
, with

(\gamma_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\mathbb{N}}
a sequence of stepsizes and

(\sigma_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\mathbb{N}}
. If
\sigma_{k}^{2}=\gamma_{k}^{2}

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{k}=+\infty
and

\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\gamma_{k}^{2}<+\infty
then the algorithm is in the

weakly disturbed
regime and under additional assumptions on
U
one can
show that
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges almost surely to a local minimizer of

U
, see
[
58
]
for instance. However,

(U(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
does not necessarily converges to the
global minimum of
U
. The intuition behind this behavior is that the variance
of the noise decreases too quickly for the sequence to explore efficiently the
landscape of
U
.

In order to perform global optimization, one can consider
simulated
annealing
algorithms where
\sigma_{k}^{2}=\gamma_{k}T_{k}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
with
(T_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{\mathbb{N}}
a sequence of temperatures which
slowly decrease. These algorithms were introduced in the context of discrete
optimization in
[
47
]
and have been thoroughly
investigated in
[
37
]
(discrete state-space),

[
33
,
34
]
(discrete-time algorithm),

[
36
,
15
,
43
]

(diffusion),
[
58
]
(CLT type results),

[
62
]
(constrained optimization),

[
72
]
(non Gaussian noise) and

[
2
]
(control of the sequence in total variation) for
instance. In
[
40
,
39
]
a sufficient and
necessary condition is given on the rate of decrease
(T_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
so
that
U(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges towards the minimum of
U
, more precisely
the condition reads
\sum_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\exp[-b^{\star}/T_{k}]=+\infty
, where

b^{\star}>0
is a parameter which depends only on
U
called the
kinetic
barrier
(or depth in
[
40
,
39
]
). One of the
main limiting factor of simulated annealing is the slow rate of convergence of

(T_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
towards
0
, which is often set as
T_{k}=C/\log(k+1)
for
any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and for some constant
C\geq 0
. Note that recently the
convergence of modifications of the simulated annealing algorithms under faster
cooling rates have been investigated in discrete spaces by either changing the
Markov chain transitions
[
16
]
or the energy landscape

[
17
]
, see also
[
13
]
. Finally, we
emphasize that most of the results regarding the convergence of
(
42
) can be extended to the case where
\nabla U
is replaced by
an unbiased estimator under additional conditions.

With the advent of neural networks, numerous schemes exploiting the annealing
structure have been proposed to minimize the non-convex losses which arise in
deep learning applications, see
[
73
]
for instance. Drawing
connections with unadjusted Langevin algorithms, and in particular Stochastic
Gradient Langevin Dynamics (SGLD)
[
68
]
,

[
60
,
75
]
replace
\nabla U
by an unbiased
estimator and let
\gamma_{k}=\gamma>0
,
\sigma_{k}^{2}=2\gamma\varepsilon
for
any
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
\varepsilon>0
in (
42
). Under curvature
and regularity assumptions on the potential
U
, the authors derive quantitative
bounds on
(\mathbb{E}[U(X_{k})-\min_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}U])_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. Since then
several accelerations have been proposed in the literature

[
14
,
32
,
27
,
55
,
74
,
71
]
.

In the next section, we improve the results of
[
60
]
by
providing upper bounds w.r.t the first order Wasserstein distance between the
distribution of
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
given by SGLD and an explicit limiting
distribution. Our results complete the ones of
[
60
]
which
deals with the behavior of

(\mathbb{E}[U(X_{k})-\min_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}U])_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. To the best of our
knowledge, this is the first result establishing quantitative bounds on the
distance between the iterates of SGLD and a limiting measure concentrated on the
minimizers of the target potential
U
.

3.2.2
Quantitative convergence for SGLD

In this section we start by recalling the setting considered in

[
60
]
. We assume that there exist a topological space

(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{Z}))
, a probability measure
\mu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{Z}))

and
u:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{Z}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for any
z\in\mathsf{Z}
,

u(\cdot,z)\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}_{+})
, and define for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{U(x)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}u(x,z)\mathrm{d}\mu(z)\;.}

(43)

We also denote
U^{\star}
the global minimum of
U
. We do not have access to

\mu
and
U
directly but instead consider an empirical version of the target.
Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We define
U_{n}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
given
for any
z^{1:n}=\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
by

\textstyle{U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})=(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}u(x,z_{i})\;.}

(44)

Let
(\mathsf{Y},\mathcal{Y})
be a measurable space,

\mathrm{R}:\ \mathsf{Z}^{n}\times\mathcal{Y}\to\left[0,1\right]
a Markov kernel and

g:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{Y}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
we have

\textstyle{\nabla_{x}U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})=\int_{\mathsf{Y}}g(x,y)\mathrm{R}(z^{1:n},\mathrm{d}y)\;.}

(45)

Let
Z
be a random variable on
\mathsf{Z}^{n}
with distribution
\mu^{\otimes n}
. Let
\{Y_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a family of independent random variables
on
\mathsf{Y}
such that conditionally to
Z
we have for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
that

Y_{k}
has distribution
\updelta_{Z}\mathrm{R}
. Finally, let
\varepsilon>0
. We
consider the Stochastic Gradient Langevin Dynamics (SGLD) sequence

(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
given by the following recursion:
X_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}

X_{k+1}=X_{k}-\gamma g(X_{k},Y_{k})+\sqrt{2\gamma\varepsilon}G_{k+1}\;,

(46)

where
(G_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of independent Gaussian random
variables with zero mean and identity covariance matrix and
\gamma>0
is a
stepsize. We also assume that
\{Y_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and

\{G_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are independent. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we denote

\mathrm{Q}_{k}:\ \mathsf{Z}^{n}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\left[0,1\right]
, the Markov kernel such
that for any random variable
Z
on
\mathsf{Z}^{n}
with distribution

\mu^{\otimes n}
, we have that
X_{k}
has distribution
\updelta_{Z}\mathrm{Q}_{k}

conditionally to
Z
. We consider the following assumption, which is
similar to the one of
[
60
]
.

H 3

(
n
)
.

For any
z\in\mathsf{Z}
,
u(\cdot,z)\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and the following hold:

(a)

There exist
A,B\geq 0
such that for any
z\in\mathsf{Z}
,
\left|u(0,z)\right|\leq A
and
\|\nabla_{x}u(0,z)\|\leq B
.

(b)

There exists
\mathtt{M}\geq 0
such that for any
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

z\in\mathsf{Z}
,

\|\nabla_{x}^{k}u(x_{1},z)-\nabla_{x}^{k}u(x_{2},z)\|\leq\mathtt{M}\|x_{1}-x_{2}\|
for any
k\in\{1,2,3\}
.

(c)

There exist
\mathtt{m}>0
,
\mathtt{c}\geq 0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

z\in\mathsf{Z}
,

\langle\nabla_{x}u(x,z),x\rangle\geq\mathtt{m}\|x\|^{2}-\mathtt{c}
.

(d)

There exists
C\geq 0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
we have

\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\int_{\mathsf{Y}}\|\nabla_{x}U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})-g(x,y)\|^{2}\mathrm{R}(z^{1:n},\mathrm{d}y)\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})\leq C(1+\|x\|^{2})\;.}

(47)

Similar to
[
60
]
we define the uniform spectral gap

\textstyle{\lambda^{\star}=\inf\{\updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{R}[\|\nabla h\|^{2}]/\updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{R}[|h|^{2}]\,:\;z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n},\ h\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\cap\mathrm{L}^{2}(\updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{R}),\ h\neq 0,\ \updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{R}[h]=0\}\;,}

(48)

and note that under
3
(
n
),
\lambda^{\star}>0
. Finally, we
also consider the following assumption which ensures that the limiting measures
we consider are well-defined.

H 4

(
n
)
.

u\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{Z},\mathbb{R})
and the following hold:

(a)

\mathsf{Z}
is compact.

(b)

There exist
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{x_{0}^{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathsf{Z}^{n},\mathbb{R}^{d})

such that for any
z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
,

\mathsf{C}(z^{1:n})\subset\{x_{0}^{k}(z)\}_{k=1}^{N}
and

\mathsf{C}(z^{1:n})\neq\emptyset
, where

\mathsf{C}(z^{1:n})=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
.

(c)

For any
\upbeta>0
,

\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\sigma_{\upbeta}^{\star}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})<+\infty
, where

\textstyle{\sigma_{\upbeta}^{\star}(z^{1:n})=\sum_{x\in\mathsf{C}(z^{1:n})}\det(\nabla_{x}^{2}U(x,z^{1:n}))^{-\upbeta}\;.}

(49)

We also introduce the
thermodynamic barrier

c^{\star}:\ \mathsf{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}

such that for any
z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
,
c^{\star}(z^{1:n})=+\infty
if

U_{n}(\cdot,z^{1:n})
does not admit a local minimizer which is not a global
minimizer and

c^{\star}(z^{1:n})=\inf\{U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})\,:\;\text{$x$ is a local minimizer of $U_{n}(\cdot,z)$ but not a global minimizer}\}-U_{n}^{\star}(z^{1:n})\;,

(50)

with
U_{n}^{\star}(z^{1:n})=\inf\{U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
. We
refer to
Section

3.2.3
for a discussion on the
thermodynamic barrier
and
its importance in non-convex optimization. Under
3
(
n
) and

4
(
n
), we derive quantitative bounds for the sequence

(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.

Proposition 8
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume
3
(
n
)
and

4
(
n
)
. Then there exist
C\geq 0
,

\bar{\varepsilon},\bar{\gamma},\upbeta>0
and
k_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any

\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]
,
\gamma\in(0,\bar{\gamma}]

and
k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k\geq k_{0}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}_{1}(\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{Q}_{k},\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0})

\displaystyle\leq C(1/\varepsilon+d)^{2}(\gamma^{1/4}\log(1/\gamma)+\gamma)/(\lambda^{\star}\varepsilon)

(51)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\textstyle{+C(1+D_{n})(\varepsilon^{1/2}+\varepsilon^{-d/2}\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{1:n}}\exp[-c^{\star}(z^{1:n})/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n}))\;,}

(52)

with

D_{n}=\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\sigma_{\upbeta}^{\star}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})<+\infty
and

\mathrm{S}_{0}:\ \mathsf{Z}^{n}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\left[0,1\right]
the Markov kernel
given for any
z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
and
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by

\textstyle{\mathrm{S}_{0}(z^{1:n},\mathsf{A})=\left.\int_{\mathsf{C}(z^{1:n})\cap\mathsf{A}}\det(\nabla_{x}^{2}U_{n}(x,z^{1:n}))^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{0}(x)\middle/\int_{\mathsf{C}(z^{1:n})}\det(\nabla_{x}^{2}U_{n}(x,z^{1:n}))^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{0}(x)\right.\;,}

(53)

with
\mathsf{C}(z^{1:n})=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
.

Proof.

We provide a sketch of the proof. The whole proof is postponed to

Section

4.3.1
. First, we assess the convergence of

(\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{Q}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
by splitting the error in two parts. A first
part is bounded using the geometric ergodicity of SGLD as in

[
60
]
and controls the distance between
\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{Q}_{k}
and

\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}
, where for any
z\in z^{1:n}
,

\updelta_{z}^{1:n}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}
is the Gibbs distribution given for any

\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\textstyle{\updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{A})=\left.\int_{\mathsf{A}}\exp[-U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\middle/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\right.\;.}

(54)

Then, using a parametric version of
Theorem

1
, see

Proposition

B.5
, we bound the distance between

\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}
. ∎

A few remarks are in order:

(a)

The condition that

\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\upsigma_{1}^{\star}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})<+\infty
is necessary to ensure that
\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}
is
well-defined. In
Section

3.2.2
we assume the slightly stronger
condition that

\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\upsigma_{\upbeta}^{\star}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})<+\infty
for any
\upbeta>0
. In fact the condition could be relaxed to

\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\upsigma_{\upbeta}^{\star}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})<+\infty
for any
\upbeta\in\left(0,\upgamma\right]
with
\upgamma>0
an
explicit constant. Obtaining such a result requires to derive explicit bounds
in the quantitative Morse lemma
[
51
, Theorem 3.2]
, see also

Proposition

B.3
. We leave this analysis to future
works. Note also that this condition can be satisfied upon regularizing the
function
u
and assuming that the number of global minimizers is
bounded. Indeed, if we replace
u(x,z)
by
u(x,z)+(\alpha/2)\|x\|^{2}

(with
\alpha>0
some regularization parameter) then we get that for any

\upbeta>0
,

\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\upsigma_{\upbeta}^{\star}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})\leq N_{0}\alpha^{-\upbeta d}
where
N_{0}
is an upper-bound on the number of global
minimizers.

(b)

The upper-bound in (
51
) also depends on the

thermodynamic barrier
. This constant quantifies how close the local
minima which are not global minima are to the global minima and is
crucial to establish quantitative parametric Laplace-type results. We
illustrate this situation in
Section

3.2.3
with a simple
example.

(c)

Section

3.2.2
ensures that for a given precision level

r>0
, there exist
\varepsilon,\gamma>0
small enough and
k\in\mathbb{N}

large enough such that

\mathbf{W}_{1}(\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{Q}_{k},\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0})\leq r
. However, note that
\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}
is not concentrated on the
minimizers of
U
. This highlights the fact that the minimization of the
empirical risk does not guarantee that the population risk is small.

In order to verify that the population risk is small in expectation
w.r.t. the target measure
\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}
we use stability tools to establish
the following proposition.

Proposition 9
.

Assume that
3
(
n
)
and

4
(
n
)
hold uniformly w.r.t
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In
addition, assume that there exist
C_{0},\alpha,\bar{\varepsilon}>0
such that for
any
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}]

\textstyle{\varepsilon^{-d/2}\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{1:n}}\exp[-c^{\star}(z^{1:n})/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})}\leq C_{0}\varepsilon^{\alpha}\;.

(55)

Then for any
\eta\in\left(0,1\right)
, there exist
C_{\eta}\geq 0
and
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
n\geq n_{0}
we have

\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U]-U^{\star}\leq C_{\eta}n^{-s\eta/(1+\eta s)}\;,

(56)

with
s=\min(1/4,\alpha/2)
and where we recall that
U^{\star}
is the global minimum of
U
.

Proof.

The proof is postponed to
Section

4.3.2
.
∎

Note that
Section

3.2.2
implies that the sequence

(\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight since under

3
(
n
) we have that there exist
R\geq 0
and

\upalpha>0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|x\|\geq R

U(x)\geq\mathtt{m}\|x\|^{\upalpha}
. Furthermore,
Section

3.2.2

implies that each limiting point of the sequence

(\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is concentrated on the minimizers
of
U
when
n\to+\infty
,
i.e.
when the number of training points

\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
grows to infinity. However, we do not necessarily have that

(\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U])_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges towards
\pi_{0}
given
for any
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by

\textstyle{\pi_{0}(\mathsf{A})=\int_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{C}}\det(\nabla^{2}U(x))^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{0}(x)/\int_{\mathsf{C}}\det(\nabla^{2}U(x))^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{0}(x)}\;,

(57)

where
\mathsf{C}=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{U(x)\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
.

In what follows, we describe a counter-example for which

(\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
does not converges weakly towards

\pi_{0}
. Let
\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{R}
,
\mathsf{Z}=\left[-1/2,1/2\right]
and

u:\ \mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
and

z\in\left[-1/2,1/2\right]
we have

\textstyle{u(x,z)=\left\{\begin{matrix}[l]&(x+\uppi)^{4}/(1+(x+\uppi)^{2})+\cos(3x)+zx\;,&\text{if $x<-\uppi\;,$}\\
&\cos(3x)+zx\;,&\text{if $-\uppi\leq x\leq\uppi\;,$}\\
&(x-\uppi)^{4}/(1+(x-\uppi)^{2})+\cos(3x)+zx\;,&\text{if $x>\uppi\;.$}\\
\end{matrix}\right.}

(58)

We also let
\mu
to be the uniform measure on
[-1/2,1/2]
.
We obtain that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
and
x\in\mathsf{X}

\textstyle{U(x)=u(x,0)\;,\qquad U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})=u(x,(1/n)\sum_{k=1}^{n}z^{k})\;.}

(59)

Note that for any
z\neq 0
,
x\mapsto u(x,z)
admits a unique global
minimizer, see the proof of
Section

3.2.2
, whereas if
z=0
,

x\mapsto u(x,z)
admits four global minimizers

\{x_{i}^{\star}\}_{i=1}^{4}=\{-\uppi,-\uppi/3,\uppi/3,\uppi\}
, see

Figure

4
for an illustration. In the next proposition, we show that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U]=U^{\star}
but that

(\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
does
not
converge weakly
towards
\pi_{0}
.

Proposition 10
.

Let
u
be given by (
58
) and
\mu
be the uniform measure
on
\left[-1/2,1/2\right]
. Then, we have that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

\textstyle{\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}=(\updelta_{-\uppi}+\updelta_{\uppi})/2\;,\qquad\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U]-U^{\star}\leq(\uppi/(6\sqrt{3}))n^{-1/2}\;.}

(60)

Proof.

The proof is postponed to
Section

4.3.3
.
∎

x
y
x
y

Figure 4:
Left: the function
x\mapsto u(x,0)
with
u
given in
(
58
). Right: the function
x\mapsto u(x,0.5)
. The global
minimizers are given by the dotted red lines.

In particular, we have that
(\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
does not
converge weakly towards
\pi_{0}
but converges towards a limiting
probability measure supported on the set of minimizers of
U
.

3.2.3
The importance of the
thermodynamic barrier

To conclude this section, we investigate the role of the
thermodynamic barrier
in order
to establish quantitative parametric Laplace-type results. This quantity should
not be confused with the concept of
kinetic barrier
which has been
investigated in the context of simulated annealing, see

[
40
,
39
]
for instance. We refer to

[
67
]
for an introduction to the concept of
thermodynamic barrier
and

kinetic barrier
in the context of chemistry, see also

Figure

5
for an illustration.

In a general setting, we
consider a function
f:\ \mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
where
\mathsf{X}
and
\mathsf{Z}

are topological spaces and
f(\cdot,z)
admits a global minimizer for any

z\in\mathsf{Z}
. Let
z\in\mathsf{Z}
, if
f(\cdot,z)
admits a local minimizer which
is not a global minimizer we recall that the
thermodynamic barrier

c^{\star}(z)
is given
by

c^{\star}(z)=\inf\{f(x,z)\,:\;\text{$x$ is a local minimizer of $f(\cdot,z)$ but not a global minimizer}\}-f^{\star}(z)\;,

(61)

with
f^{\star}(z)=\inf\{f(x,z)\,:\;x\in\mathsf{X}\}
. The
thermodynamic barrier
quantifies how close the values of the local minima are to the global
ones. Let
\mathsf{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for any
\varepsilon>0
,
z\in\mathsf{Z}
and
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, define

\displaystyle\textstyle{\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{A})=\int_{\mathsf{A}}\exp[-f(x,z)/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-f(x,z)/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\;,}

(62)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0}(\mathsf{A})=\int_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{C}(z)}\det(\nabla_{x}^{2}f(x,z))^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{0}(x)/\int_{\mathsf{C}(z)}\det(\nabla_{x}^{2}f(x,z))^{-1/2}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{0}(x),}

(63)

with
\mathsf{C}(z)=\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{f(x,z)\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
. In

Proposition

B.5
we show (under assumptions on
f
) that for
any
z\in\mathsf{Z}
and for any
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
which satisfies
the conditions of
Proposition

B.5
there exist

A,\upbeta\geq 0
and
\bar{\varepsilon}>0
such that for any

\varepsilon\in\left(0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right)

\left|\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}[\varphi]-\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0}[\varphi]\right|\leq A(1+\sigma_{\upbeta}^{\star}(z))\{\varepsilon^{1/2}+\varepsilon^{-d/2}\exp[-c^{\star}(z)/\varepsilon]\}\;,

(64)

with
A,\upbeta
independent from
z
. The dependency of the right-hand
side w.r.t
\sigma_{\upbeta}^{\star}(z)
comes from the fact that

\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0}
is well-defined if and only if
\nabla_{x}^{2}f(x,z)
is
invertible at the global minimizers of
f(\cdot,z)
.

We now investigate the dependency w.r.t the
thermodynamic barrier

c^{\star}
. We are
going to build a simple example for which the
thermodynamic barrier
plays a
crucial role. In particular, we will show that the dependency of the form

\exp[-c^{\star}(z)/\varepsilon]
is tight in (
64
). Let

k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathsf{X}=\{0,1\}
,
\mathsf{Z}=\mathbb{R}
and

f:\ \mathsf{X}\times\mathsf{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for any
x\in\mathsf{X}
and

z\in\mathsf{Z}
,
f(x,z)=xz^{2k+1}
. For any
\varepsilon>0
and

z\in\mathbb{R}
we define
\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}
by

\displaystyle\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=(\updelta_{0}\exp[-f(0,z)/\varepsilon]+\updelta_{1}\exp[-f(1,z)/\varepsilon])/(1+\exp[-f(1,z)/\varepsilon])

(65)

\displaystyle=\updelta_{0}\mathrm{sigm}(z^{2k+1}/\varepsilon)+\updelta_{1}\mathrm{sigm}(-z^{2k+1}/\varepsilon)\;,

(66)

where
\mathrm{sigm}:\ \mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is the sigmoid function given for any

t\in\mathbb{R}
by
\mathrm{sigm}(t)=(1+\exp[-t])^{-1}
. When
z>0
the
minimum of
f(\cdot,z)
is
0
and is attained at
x=0
. When
z=0
, we
have
f=0
(and the minimum is therefore attained at
x=0
and

x=1
). When
z<0
the minimum of
f(\cdot,z)
is
-z^{2k+1}
and is
attained at
x=1
. Therefore we have that

\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0}=\updelta_{0}
if
z>0
,

\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0}=\updelta_{1}
if
z<0
and

\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0}=(\updelta_{0}+\updelta_{1})/2
if
z=0
. Using that
the Wasserstein distance of order
1
between two Bernoulli distribution
with parameter
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
is given by
|p_{1}-p_{2}|
we get
that for any
z\in\mathsf{Z}
and
\varepsilon>0

\mathbf{W}_{1}(\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon},\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0})=\mathrm{sigm}(-\left|z\right|^{2k+1}/\varepsilon)\;.

(67)

Hence, for a
fixed
value of
z\in\mathsf{Z}
, we get that

\mathbf{W}_{1}(\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon},\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0})
is of
order
\mathcal{O}(\exp[-\left|z\right|^{2k+1}/\varepsilon])
. In particular, we get that
for any
z\in\mathsf{Z}
,

\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbf{W}_{1}(\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon},\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0})/\varepsilon
is bounded. In what follows, we show that

\liminf_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbf{W}_{1}(\mu\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon},\mu\mathrm{S}_{0})/\varepsilon=+\infty
, for some probability measure

\mu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R})
.

Let
\chi:\ \mathsf{X}\to\mathbb{R}
with
\chi(0)=1
and
\chi(1)=0
. First, note that
for any
z>0
we have

\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}[\chi]-\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0}[\chi]=\mathrm{sigm}(-z^{2k+1}/\varepsilon)\;.

(68)

Hence, using that
c^{\star}(z)=\left|z\right|^{2k+1}
for any
z\in\mathbb{R}
and that

\mathrm{sigm}(-t)\geq\exp[-2t]
for any
t>0
we have for any
z>0
,

\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}[\chi]-\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0}[\chi]\geq\exp[-2z^{2k+1}/\varepsilon]\geq\exp[-2c^{\star}(z)/\varepsilon]\;.

(69)

Let
\mu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that
\mu(\{0\})=0
. Using
(
69
) and that
\chi
is
1
-Lipschitz, we have

\textstyle{\mathbf{W}_{1}(\mu\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon},\mu\mathrm{S}_{0})\geq\int_{0}^{+\infty}\exp[-2c^{\star}(z)/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}\mu(z)\;.}

(70)

Assume that
\mu
is the uniform distribution on
\left[0,1\right]
. Then, we
have for any
\varepsilon\in\left(0,1\right)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathbf{W}_{1}(\mu\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon},\mu\mathrm{S}_{0})}\geq\textstyle{\int_{0}^{1}\exp[-2z^{2k+1}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\geq 2^{-1/(2k+1)}(\int_{0}^{1}\exp[-z^{2k+1}]\mathrm{d}z)\varepsilon^{1/(2k+1)}\;.}

(71)

This shows that the order of

\mathbf{W}_{1}(\mu\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon},\mu\mathrm{S}_{0})
is at most

\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{1/(2k+1)})
. This is in stark contrast with the order
identified for

\mathbf{W}_{1}(\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon},\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0})
.

This latter observation highlights the crucial role of the
thermodynamic barrier
when establishing uniform Laplace-type results w.r.t some parameter

z\in\mathsf{Z}
. Note that we recover that

\mathbf{W}_{1}(\mu\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon},\mu\mathrm{S}_{0})
is of order at least

\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon)
if
\mu
is supported on
\left[z_{0},+\infty\right)
with

z_{0}>0
. Similar conclusions hold if we show that
c^{\star}(z)\geq c_{0}

for any
z\in\mathbb{R}
with
c_{0}>0
. Hence, (assuming that
c^{\star}
is
continuous) the discrepancy between the order of

\mathbf{W}_{1}(\mu\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon},\mu\mathrm{S}_{0})
and the one of

\mathbf{W}_{1}(\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon},\updelta_{z}\mathrm{S}_{0})
might
arise if:

(a)

At least one of the local minima (which is not a global minimum)
converges towards a global minimum when
z\to z^{\star}
for some value of

z^{\star}\in\mathsf{Z}
,

(b)

z^{\star}
belongs to the support of
\mu
.

If these two conditions are fulfilled then a more careful study of

\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\exp[-c^{\star}(z)/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}\mu(z)
is needed in order to obtain
quantitative bounds.

x
y

Figure 5:
Difference between the
thermodynamic barrier
(blue) and the
kinetic barrier
(red).

4
Proofs

In this section, we gather the proofs of the previous sections. In

Section

4.1
we prove
Theorem

3
. Then, in

Section

4.2
we provide the proofs of the results of

Section

3.1
. Finally, the proofs of the results of

Section

3.2
are given in
Section

4.3
.

4.1
Proof of
Theorem

3

In this section, we prove
Theorem

3
. We recall that

Theorem

1
is a straightforward consequence of

Theorem

3
upon letting
\Psi=1
. We let
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
,

F:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\Psi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
. For any

\varphi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\varepsilon>0
we define

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)=C_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\Psi(x)\exp[-\left\|F(x)\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\;,\quad\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(1)\;,}

(72)

\displaystyle\textstyle{C_{\varepsilon}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x=\varepsilon^{d/k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}x=\varepsilon^{d/k}C_{1}\;.}

(73)

In addition, we define

\textstyle{\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)=\int_{F^{-1}(0)}\varphi(x)\Psi(x)\mathrm{J}F(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-\hat{d}}(x)\;,\quad\mathcal{J}_{0}=\mathcal{I}_{0}(1)\;,}

(74)

where
\hat{d}=\min(d,p)
and we recall that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\mathrm{J}F(x)=\det(\mathrm{D}F(x)\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top})^{1/2}
if
d\geq p
and

\mathrm{J}F(x)=\det(\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top}\mathrm{D}F(x))^{1/2}
otherwise. If

\varphi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
I_{\varepsilon}(\left|\varphi\right|)<+\infty
for
some
\varepsilon\geq 0
we define
\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)
similarly as in
(
72
) and (
74
). Note that for any

\varepsilon\geq 0
and
\varphi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
such that it is defined we
have
\pi_{\varepsilon}[\varphi]=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)/\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}
.

The rest of this section is organized as follows. In
Section

4.1.1
, we
prove our main result ,
i.e.
a quantitative Laplace-type result in the case

d\geq p
using the coarea formula. In
Section

4.1.2
, we prove similar
results in the case
d\leq p
using Laplace’s method and Morse theory. Finally,
we conclude with the proof of
Theorem

3
in

Section

4.1.3
. Additional technical results are postponed to

Appendix

A
.

4.1.1
The case
d\geq p

In what follows, we assume that
d\geq p
and for any

\varphi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
,
t\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
we define
\mathcal{L}_{t}(\varphi)
by

\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{t}(\varphi)=\int_{F^{-1}(t)}\varphi(x)\Psi(x)\mathrm{J}F(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-p}(x)\;.}

(75)

Note that
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)
. Let

\varphi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then, if
\mathcal{L}_{t}(\left|\varphi\right|)<+\infty
, we
define
\mathcal{L}_{t}(\varphi)
similarly to (
75
). The following proposition
establishes that
t\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{t}(\varphi)
is Lipschitz under mild regularity
conditions. We emphasize that this proposition is no longer true if

d\leq p
. Indeed, let us consider the following counterexample. Let

F:\ \mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
given for any
x\in\mathbb{R}
by

F(x)=((1-x^{2})/(1+x^{2}),x(1-x^{2})/(1+x^{2}))
. The set
F(\mathbb{R})
defines a right
strophoid. Then, for any
t\neq 0
we have
\mathcal{H}^{0}(F^{-1}(t))=1
or
0
, but

\mathcal{H}^{0}(F^{-1}(0))=2
and therefore
\mathcal{L}_{t}(\mathrm{J}F/\Psi)=1
or
0

near
t=0
but
\mathcal{L}_{0}(\mathrm{J}F/\Psi)=2
with
\Psi=1
. Hence

t\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{t}(\mathrm{J}F/\Psi)
is not even continuous.

Proposition 11
.

Assume
1
,
2
and that
d\geq p
. Let

\mathsf{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
open such that
