F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{U}
and

\varphi\in\mathrm{C}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})
. Then

\lim_{t\to 0}\mathcal{L}_{t}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}_{0}(\varphi)
. In addition, assume that

\Psi,\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})
. Then there exist
B_{0}\geq 0

and
\eta>0
such that for any
t\in\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,\eta)
,

\left|\mathcal{L}_{t}(\varphi)-\mathcal{L}_{0}(\varphi)\right|\leq B_{0}(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})\left\|t\right\|\;,

(76)

with for any
i\in\{0,1\}
and
f\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,

M_{i,f}=\sup\{\|\nabla^{i}f(x)\|\,:\;x\in F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,\eta))\}
,
B_{0}
and
\eta
are independent from
\varphi
and

\Psi
and
F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,\eta))\subset\mathsf{U}
.

Proof.

First, we show that there exists an explicit diffeomorphism between

F^{-1}(t)
and
F^{-1}(0)
for
\|t\|
small enough. Then we use the
coarea formula to express
\mathcal{L}_{t}
as an integral over
F^{-1}(0)
and use the
dominated convergence theorem to conclude the first part of the proof. For the
second part of the proof we differentiate the diffeomorphism w.r.t the
parameter
t
and provide explicit bounds for the derivative.

(a)

The set
F^{-1}(0)
is compact since

\lim_{\left\|x\right\|\to+\infty}\left\|F(x)\right\|=+\infty
. First, there exists

\eta_{0}>0
such that
F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,\eta_{0}))\subset\mathsf{U}
. Indeed,
since
F^{-1}(0)
is compact, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that

F^{-1}(0)+\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,\varepsilon_{0})\subset\mathsf{U}
. We now show that for any

\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,\eta_{\varepsilon}))\subset F^{-1}(0)+\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,\varepsilon)
. If this is false, we let
\varepsilon>0
such that
for any
\eta>0
,

F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,\eta))\not\subset F^{-1}(0)+\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,\varepsilon)
. Hence there exists a sequence

(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\|F(x_{k})\|_{\infty}\leq 1/(k+1)
and

d(x_{k},F^{-1}(0))\geq\varepsilon
. But, up to extraction, there exists

x^{\star}\in F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,1))
such that

\lim_{k\to+\infty}x_{k}=x^{\star}
. Then, we have
F(x^{\star})=0
and

d(x^{\star},F^{-1}(0))>\varepsilon
, which is absurd. Hence for any

\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\eta_{\varepsilon}>0
such that

F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,\eta_{\varepsilon}))\subset F^{-1}(0)+\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,\varepsilon)
. We let
\eta_{0}=\eta_{\varepsilon_{0}}
.

Second, there exists
\eta_{1}>0
such that for any

x\in F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,\eta_{1}))
,
\mathrm{J}F(x)>0
. Indeed, if this is
not the case then there exists
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with

\lim_{k\to+\infty}F(x_{k})=0
and
\mathrm{J}F(x_{k})=0
. Since

F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,1))
is compact there exists
x^{\star}
such that, up
to extraction,
\lim_{k\to+\infty}x_{k}=x^{\star}
. Then
F(x^{\star})=0
and

\mathrm{J}F(x^{\star})=0
, which is absurd. We define

\mathsf{K}_{0}=F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,\eta_{1}))
and

\mathsf{K}_{1}=F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,\eta))
with

\eta=\min(\eta_{1}/2,\eta_{0})
. Note that
\mathsf{K}_{1}\subset\mathsf{U}
and

\mathsf{K}_{1}\subset\mathrm{int}(\mathsf{K}_{0})
.

In what follows for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we define

G(x)=\mathrm{D}F(x)\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top}
. Note that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\det(G(x))^{1/2}=\mathrm{J}F(x)
since
d\geq p
. We also have that for any

x\in\mathsf{K}_{0}
,
G(x)
is invertible since
\mathrm{J}F(x)>0
. In addition, we
have that for any
i,j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

G_{i,j}(x)=\langle\nabla F_{i}(x),\nabla F_{j}(x)\rangle\;.

(77)

We define
\{f_{i}\}_{i=1}^{p}
such that for any
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
,

f_{i}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
with for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{0}

\textstyle{f_{i}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{p}h_{i,k}(x)\nabla F_{k}(x)\;,}

(78)

with
\{h_{i,j}(x)\}_{1\leq i,j\leq p}=G(x)^{-1}
.
For any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{0}
and
i,j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
we have

\textstyle{\langle f_{i}(x),\nabla F_{j}(x)\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{p}h_{i,k}(x)\langle\nabla F_{k}(x),\nabla F_{j}(x)\rangle=\updelta_{i}(j)\;,}

(79)

where
\updelta_{i}
is the Dirac mass at
i
. In what follows, we let

\{g_{i}\}_{i=1}^{p}
such that for any
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
,

g_{i}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
g_{i}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
such
that
g_{i}(x)=f_{i}(x)
for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
, and
g_{i}(x)=0
for

x\in\mathrm{int}(\mathsf{K}_{0})^{\mathrm{c}}
, such functions exist using
Whitney extension theorem for instance, see
[
69
]
. In
what follows, we fix
t=(t_{1},\dots,t_{p})\in\bar{\operatorname{B}}_{\infty}(0,\eta)
. For
any
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
let
\Phi_{i}:\ \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}

given by
\Phi_{i}(0,x)=x
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and for any

s\in\mathbb{R}
and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\partial_{s}\Phi_{i}(s,x)=-g_{i}(\Phi_{i}(s,x))\;.

(80)

For any
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
,
\Phi_{i}
is well-defined using

Lemma

C.1
. Therefore, we have for any
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and

s\in\mathbb{R}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\Phi_{i}(s,x)\in\mathsf{K}_{1}

\partial_{s}F(\Phi_{i}(s,x))=-(\langle g_{i}(\Phi_{i}(s,x)),\nabla F_{1}(\Phi_{i}(s,x))\rangle,\dots,\langle g_{i}(\Phi_{i}(s,x)),\nabla F_{p}(\Phi_{i}(s,x))\rangle)=-e_{i}\;,

(81)

where we recall that
\{e_{i}\}_{i=1}^{p}
is the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
We define
\bar{\Phi}_{t}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that for any

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\bar{\Phi}_{t}(x)=x^{(p)}
with
x^{(0)}=x
and for any

i\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}
,
x^{(i+1)}=\Phi_{i+1}(t_{i+1},x^{(i)})
. Note that

\bar{\Phi}_{t}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
and is a diffeomorphism, see

Lemma

C.1
. Using (
80
) we have that

\bar{\Phi}_{t}(F^{-1}(t))=F^{-1}(0)
. In addition,
F^{-1}(t)
is

\mathcal{H}^{d-p}
countably rectifiable using

Lemma

C.5
. Using this result and the coarea
formula, see
Theorem

C.6
, we have

\textstyle{\mathcal{L}_{t}(\varphi)=\int_{F^{-1}(0)}\varphi(\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x))\Psi(\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x))\mathrm{J}F(\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x))^{-1}|\det(\mathrm{D}\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x))|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-p}(x)\;.}

(82)

Since
F^{-1}(0)\times\operatorname{B}_{\infty}(0,\eta)
is compact and

(t,x)\mapsto\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x)
and

(t,x)\mapsto\mathrm{D}\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x)
are continuous with for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{-1}(x)=x
and
\mathrm{D}\bar{\Phi}_{0}^{-1}(x)=\operatorname{Id}
, we get
that
\lim_{t\to 0}\mathcal{L}_{t}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}_{0}(\varphi)
using the dominated
convergence theorem.

(b)

For the second part of the proof we control the derivative of

t\mapsto\chi(t,x)
where for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
we have

\chi(t,x)=\varphi(\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x))\Psi(\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1})\mathrm{J}F(\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x))^{-1}|\det(\mathrm{D}\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x))|\;.

(83)

Using
Lemma

A.5
, there exists

P\in\mathtt{Poly}(4,\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that for
x\in F^{-1}(0)
and

t\in\operatorname{B}_{\infty}(0,\eta)
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{t}\chi(t,x)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})

(84)

\displaystyle\quad\times P(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})\exp[P(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})]\;,

(85)

with
P
independent from
\varphi
and
\Psi
.
Hence we have that for any
t\in\operatorname{B}_{\infty}(0,\eta)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{L}_{t}(\varphi)-\mathcal{L}_{0}(\varphi)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\int_{F^{-1}(0)}\left|\chi(t,x)-\chi(0,x)\right|\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-p}(x)}

(86)

\displaystyle\leq(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})P(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})

(87)

\displaystyle\qquad\exp[P(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})]\mathcal{H}^{d-p}(F^{-1}(0))\left\|t\right\|\;,

(88)

which concludes the proof since
\mathcal{H}^{d-p}(F^{-1}(0))<+\infty
by
Lemma

C.5
.

∎

Proposition 12
.

Assume
1
,
2
and that
d\geq p
. Let

\mathsf{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
open and bounded such that
F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{U}
and

\varphi\in\mathrm{C}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})
. Then there exists
\eta>0
such that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)
,
where for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi\mathbbm{1}_{F^{-1}(\operatorname{B}(0,\eta))})
. In addition, assume that

\varphi,\Psi\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})
, then there exists
A_{2}\geq 0

such that for any
\varepsilon>0

\textstyle{\left|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)\right|\leq A_{2}(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})\varepsilon^{1/k}\;,}

(89)

with for any
i\in\{0,1\}
and
f\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,

M_{i,f}=\sup\{\|\nabla^{i}f(x)\|\,:\;x\in F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,\eta))\}
,
A_{2}
and
\eta
are independent from

\varphi
and
\Psi
and
F^{-1}(\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,\eta))\subset\mathsf{U}
.

Proof.

First, note that
\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}_{0}(\varphi)
, see (
74
) and
(
75
). In what follows, we let
\eta>0
be given by

Section

4.1.1
and define

\mathcal{I}_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)=C_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\int_{\operatorname{B}(0,\eta)}\exp[-\left\|t\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathcal{L}_{0}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}t
. We have

\textstyle{|\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)|=\left|\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)\right|C_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\int_{\operatorname{B}(0,\eta)^{\mathrm{c}}}\exp[-\left\|t\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}t\leq 2^{d/k}\left|\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)\right|\exp[-\eta^{k}/(2\varepsilon)]\;.}

(90)

Using the coarea formula, see
Theorem

C.6
, we have for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\textstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)=C_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\int_{F^{-1}(\operatorname{B}(0,\eta))}\Psi(x)\varphi(x)\exp[-\left\|F(x)\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]=C_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\int_{\operatorname{B}(0,\eta)}\exp[-\left\|t\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathcal{L}_{t}(\varphi)\mathrm{d}t\;.}

(91)

Therefore, using this result and the change of variable

t\mapsto\varepsilon^{1/k}t
we have

\displaystyle|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)|

\displaystyle\textstyle{\leq C_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\int_{\operatorname{B}(0,\eta)}\exp[-\left\|t\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]|\mathcal{L}_{t}(\varphi)-\mathcal{L}_{0}(\varphi)|\mathrm{d}t}

(92)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{C_{1}^{-1}\int_{\operatorname{B}(0,\eta/\varepsilon^{1/k})}\exp[-\left\|t\right\|^{k}]|\mathcal{L}_{t\varepsilon^{1/k}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{L}_{0}(\varphi)|\mathrm{d}t\;.}

(93)

Hence, we get that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)|=0
using the dominated convergence theorem and
that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}|\mathcal{L}_{t\varepsilon^{1/k}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{L}_{0}(\varphi)|=0
according to
Section

4.1.1
. This concludes the first part of the proof
upon combining this result and (
90
). In addition, assume that

\varphi\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})
then using the second part of

Section

4.1.1
and (
93
) we have

\left|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)\right|\leq\textstyle{B_{0}C_{1}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\|t\right\|\exp[-\left\|t\right\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}t(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})\varepsilon^{1/k}\;,}

(94)

which concludes the proof upon combining this result and (
90
).
∎

4.1.2
The case
d\leq p

We now turn to the case
d\leq p
. The proof of this result is more classical
and does not rely on geometric measure theory. Instead we build on the Morse
theory approach for Laplace approximation, see
[
70
]
for
example. The following proposition is a quantitative extension of
[
70
, Theorem
3, p.495]
.

Proposition 13
.

Assume
1
,
2
and that
d\leq p
. Let

\mathsf{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
open and bounded such that
F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{U}
and

\varphi\in\mathrm{C}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})
. Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)|=0
, with

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{V}})
and

\mathsf{V}
open such that
F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{V}\subset\mathsf{U}
. In addition,
assume that
\varphi,\Psi\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})
. Then there exists

B_{1}\geq 0
such that for any
\varepsilon>0
we have

|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)|\leq B_{1}(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})\varepsilon^{1/k}\;,

(95)

with
B_{1}

independent from
\varphi
and
\Psi
and for any
i\in\{0,1\}
and

f\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})
,

M_{i,f}=\sup\{\|\nabla^{i}f(x)\|\,:\;x\in\mathsf{U}\}
.

Proof.

Let
\{x_{0}^{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{N}
and
\{\mathsf{W}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{N}
be given by

Lemma

A.4
such that
F^{-1}(0)=\cup_{\ell=1}^{N}\{x_{0}^{\ell}\}

and
\mathrm{d}F(x)
is injective for any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and

x\in\mathsf{W}_{\ell}
. In addition, for any
\ell,m\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,

\mathsf{W}_{\ell}\cap\mathsf{W}_{m}=\emptyset
. Let
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and

U:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

U(x)=\|F(x)\|^{2}
. Since
F\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
we
have that
U\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
. We divide the rest of the
proof into two parts.

(a)

First, we have that

\nabla^{2}U(x_{0}^{\ell})=2\mathrm{D}F(x_{0}^{\ell})^{\top}\mathrm{D}F(x_{0}^{\ell})
which is
invertible since
\mathrm{J}F(x_{0}^{\ell})>0
. Therefore, we can apply Morse’s lemma

[
56
, Theorem 3.1.1]
and there exists a diffeormorphism

\Phi_{\ell}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\bar{\Omega}_{\ell},\bar{\mathsf{W}}_{\ell})
with

0\in\Omega_{\ell}
,
x_{0}^{\ell}\in\mathsf{W}_{\ell}
and

\Omega_{\ell}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
open such that for any
x\in\Omega_{\ell}
,

U(\Phi_{\ell}(x))=\left\|x\right\|^{2}
,
\Phi_{\ell}(0)=x_{0}^{\ell}
and

\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\ell}(0)=(\mathrm{D}F(x_{0}^{\ell})^{\top}\mathrm{D}F(x_{0}^{\ell}))^{-1/2}
. Note
that
\det(\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\ell}(0))=\mathrm{J}F(x_{0}^{\ell})^{-1}
.

Let
r_{\ell}>0
such that
\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,r_{\ell})\subset\Omega_{\ell}
. We have

\textstyle{1-\int_{\Omega_{\ell}/\varepsilon^{1/k}}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}x/C_{1}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}/2]\mathrm{d}x\exp[-r_{\ell}^{k}/(2\varepsilon)]/C_{1}\;.}

(96)

In what follows, we no longer consider
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
to be
fixed. Let
\mathsf{V}=\cup_{\ell=1}^{N}\Phi_{\ell}(\Omega_{\ell})
and

\mathcal{I}_{0,\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\int_{\Omega_{\ell}/\varepsilon^{1/k}}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}x\varphi(x_{0}^{\ell})\Psi(x_{0}^{\ell})\mathrm{J}F(x_{0}^{\ell})^{-1}/C_{1}
. We recall that we have

\textstyle{\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)=\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\varphi(x_{0}^{\ell})\Psi(x_{0}^{\ell})\mathrm{J}F(x_{0}^{\ell})^{-1}\;.}

(97)

Combining this result and (
96
) we get

\textstyle{\left|\mathcal{I}_{0,\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)\right|\leq NM\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}/2]\mathrm{d}x\exp[-r_{\mathrm{min}}^{k}/(2\varepsilon)]/C_{1}\;,}

(98)

where

r_{\mathrm{min}}=\min\{r_{\ell}\,:\;\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}\}
and

M=\max\{|\varphi(x_{0}^{\ell})|\Psi(x_{0}^{\ell})\mathrm{J}F(x_{0}^{\ell})^{-1}\,:\;\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}\}
. Using for any

\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
the change of variable
x\mapsto\Phi_{\ell}(x)
and

x\mapsto\varepsilon^{1/k}x
we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}_{0,\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)\right|

\displaystyle\textstyle{\leq\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\int_{\Omega_{\ell}/\varepsilon^{1/k}}|\varphi(\Phi_{\ell}(\varepsilon^{1/k}x))\Psi(\Phi_{\ell}(\varepsilon^{1/k}x))\det(\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\ell}(\varepsilon^{1/k}x))}

(99)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\varphi(x_{0}^{\ell})\Psi(x_{0}^{\ell})\mathrm{J}F(x_{0}^{\ell})^{-1}|\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}x/C_{1}\;,

(100)

For any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, let
\chi_{\ell}:\ \Omega_{\ell}\to\mathbb{R}

given for any
x\in\Omega_{\ell}
by

\chi_{\ell}(x)=\varphi(\Phi_{\ell}(x))\Psi(\Phi_{\ell}(x))\det(\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\ell}(x))\;.

(101)

We conclude the first part of the proof using (
98
), the
dominated convergence theorem in (
100
) and that for any

\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,
\chi\in\mathrm{C}(\Omega_{\ell},\mathbb{R})
.

(b)

For the second part of the proof, since

\varphi,\Psi\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})
and for any

\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,

\Phi_{\ell}\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\bar{\Omega}_{\ell},\bar{\mathsf{W}}_{\ell})
since

F\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
using
[
56
, Theorem
3.1.1]
, we have that for any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,

\chi_{\ell}\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\bar{\Omega}_{\ell},\mathbb{R})
and there exists

B_{1}^{\prime}\geq 0
(independent from
\varphi
and
\Psi
) such that for any

\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and
x\in\Omega_{\ell}
we have

\left\|\mathrm{D}\chi_{\ell}(x)\right\|\leq B_{1}^{\prime}(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})\;.

(102)

Using this result and (
100
) we get that

|\mathcal{I}_{0,\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)|\leq\textstyle{NB_{1}^{\prime}(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})\varepsilon^{1/k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left\|x\right\|\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}x/C_{1}\;.}

(103)

Combining this result and (
98
) concludes the proof.

∎

4.1.3
Proof of
Theorem

3

We start by proving the results of
Theorem

3
in a smooth setting, then we deduce the general case using a smoothing lemma.

Proposition 14
.

Assume
1
and

2
. Let
\mathsf{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
open and bounded such that

F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{U}
and
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})
which
satisfies (
19
). Then,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\left|\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}[\varphi]-\pi_{0}^{\Psi}[\varphi]\right|=0
. In addition, assume that
\varphi,\Psi\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})
. Then there exists
A\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{3},\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that for any

\varepsilon\in(0,\mathtt{m}^{k}/(4\{C_{\varphi}+C_{\Psi}+C_{\varphi}C_{\Psi}+1\}))

\left|\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}[\varphi]-\pi_{0}^{\Psi}[\varphi]\right|\leq A(C_{\varphi},C_{\Psi},m_{0,\Psi})(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})\varepsilon^{1/k}\;,

(104)

with for any
i\in\{0,1\}
and
f\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,

M_{i,f}=\sup\{\|\nabla^{i}f(x)\|\,:\;x\in\mathsf{U}\}
,

m_{0,\Psi}=\inf\{\Psi(x)\,:\;x\in F^{-1}(0)\}
and
A

independent function from
\varphi
and
\Psi
. Finally,
A
is non-decreasing w.r.t its
first two variables and non-increasing w.r.t its last variable.

Proof.

For any
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right)
we have

\displaystyle\left|\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}[\varphi]-\pi_{0}^{\Psi}[\varphi]\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)/\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}-\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)/\mathcal{J}_{0}\right|

(105)

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\left|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)\right|+\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)/(\mathcal{J}_{0}\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon})\left|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(1)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(1)\right|\;.

(106)

Let
\eta>0
be given by
Section

4.1.1
and let

\mathsf{V}=F^{-1}(\operatorname{B}(0,\eta))
if
d\geq p
, and
\mathsf{V}
given by

Section

4.1.2
otherwise. Note that

F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{V}
. For any
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right)
we define

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{out}}(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{c}}})
and

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{V}})
. Let

\bar{\varepsilon}=\mathtt{m}^{k}/(4+4C_{\varphi,\Psi})
, with

C_{\varphi,\Psi}=(C_{\Psi}+1)(C_{\varphi}+1)
and note that we have

\bar{\varepsilon}<\min(\mathtt{m}^{k}/(1+C_{\varphi,\Psi}),\mathtt{m}^{k}/(1+C_{\varphi,1}))\;.

(107)

We divide the rest of the proof into two parts. First, we control

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{out}}(\varphi)
using the technical bounds of

Lemma

A.3
. Then, we control

|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)|
using either

Section

4.1.1
if
d\geq p
or
Section

4.1.2

if
d\leq p
. We conclude upon combining these results.

(a)

Using (
107
) and
Lemma

A.3
we get that
for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon})
we have

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{out}}(\varphi)\leq A_{1}(C_{\varphi,\Psi})\varepsilon^{-d/k}\exp[-\beta_{1}/\varepsilon]\;,\qquad\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{out}}(1)\leq A_{1}(C_{1,\Psi})\varepsilon^{-d/k}\exp[-\beta_{1}/\varepsilon]\;,

(108)

with
\beta_{1}>0
and
A_{1}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
independent from

\varphi
and
\Psi
and non-decreasing.

(b)

Using either
Section

4.1.1
if
d\geq p
or

Section

4.1.2
if
d\leq p
we have that for any

\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)\right|\leq A_{2}(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})\varepsilon^{1/k}\;,

(109)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(1)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(1)\right|\leq 2A_{2}(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})\varepsilon^{1/k}\;,

(110)

with for any
i\in\{0,1\}
and
f\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
,

M_{i,f}=\sup\{\|\nabla^{i}f(x)\|\,:\;x\in\mathsf{U}\}
,
A_{2}

independent from
\varphi
and
\Psi
.

Combining (
108
) and
(
109
) we get that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)\right|\leq A_{2}(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})\varepsilon^{1/k}+A_{1}(C_{\varphi,\Psi})\varepsilon^{-d/k}\exp[-\beta_{1}/\varepsilon]\;,

(111)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(1)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(1)\right|\leq 2A_{2}(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})\varepsilon^{1/k}+A_{1}(C_{1,\Psi})\varepsilon^{-d/k}\exp[-\beta_{1}/\varepsilon]\;.

(112)

Since for any
\varepsilon>0
,
\varepsilon^{-d/k}\exp[-\beta/\varepsilon]\leq(1/(k\beta)+k/(d\beta))^{1/k+d/k}\varepsilon^{1/k}
there exists
\tilde{A}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2},\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that for any
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon})
we have

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)\right|\leq\tilde{A}(C_{\varphi},C_{\Psi})(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})\varepsilon^{1/k}\;,

(113)

\displaystyle\left|\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(1)-\mathcal{I}_{0}(1)\right|\leq 2\tilde{A}(1,C_{\Psi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})\varepsilon^{1/k}\;,

(114)

with
\tilde{A}
independent from
\Psi
and
\varphi
and
\tilde{A}

non-decreasing w.r.t. to each of its variables. Using

Lemma

A.2
, there exists

A_{0}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+}^{\star})
such that for any

\varepsilon\in\left[0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right]
,
\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}\geq A_{0}(m_{0,\Psi})
with

m_{0,\Psi}=\inf\{\Psi(x)\,:\;x\in F^{-1}(0)\}
and
A_{0}

independent from
\Psi
and non-increasing. Finally, note that

\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)\leq\inf\{\mathrm{J}F(x)^{-1}\,:\;x\in F^{-1}(0)\}M_{0,\varphi}\mathcal{H}^{d-p}(F^{-1}(0))
with
\mathcal{H}^{d-p}(F^{-1}(0))<+\infty
by

Lemma

C.5
. Combining these results, (
105
)
and (
113
) concludes the proof.
∎

Using this proposition along with a smoothing lemma, see

Lemma

A.6
, we conclude the proof of

Theorem

3
.

Proof of
Theorem

3
.

We begin by introducing the families

\{\varphi^{\delta}\,:\;\delta\in\left(0,\bar{\delta}\right)\}
and

\{\Psi^{\delta}\,:\;\delta\in\left(0,\bar{\delta}\right)\}
which are smooth
approximations of
\varphi
and
\Psi
respectively. Since
F^{-1}(0)
is
compact and
\mathsf{U}^{\mathrm{c}}
is closed there exists
r>0
such that

F^{-1}(0)+\operatorname{B}(0,r)\subset\mathsf{U}
. Let

\mathsf{U}_{0}=F^{-1}(0)+\operatorname{B}(0,r/2)
and remark that

F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{U}_{0}
and
\mathsf{U}_{0}+\operatorname{B}(0,r/2)\subset\mathsf{U}
. Let

\{\varphi^{\delta}\,:\;\delta\in\left(0,\bar{\delta}\right)\}
and

\{\Psi^{\delta}\,:\;\delta\in\left(0,\bar{\delta}\right)\}
with

\bar{\delta}>0
given by
Lemma

A.6
,
\mathsf{V}\leftarrow\mathsf{U}

and
\mathsf{U}\leftarrow\mathsf{U}_{0}
. Then using the dominated convergence theorem and
the fact that
F^{-1}(0)
is compact we have that

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\pi_{0}^{\Psi^{\delta}}[\varphi^{\delta}]=\pi_{0}^{\Psi}[\varphi]
. Similarly, using the dominated convergence theorem we get that there exists

\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that for any

\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}(C_{\varphi}))
we have

\lim_{\delta\to 0}\pi^{\Psi^{\delta}}_{\varepsilon}[\varphi^{\delta}]=\pi^{\Psi}_{\varepsilon}[\varphi]
. We
conclude upon using
Section

4.1.3
and

Lemma

A.6
.

∎

4.2
Proofs of
Section

3.1

In this section, we prove
Section

3.1.2
. Similarly to the proof of

Theorem

3
we divide the proof into two parts depending on
whether
d\geq p
in
Section

4.2.1
or
d\leq p
in

Section

4.2.2
. Our main result, which is a generalization of

Section

3.1.2
is presented in
Section

4.2.3
. We define

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}
and
\mathcal{I}_{0}
as in (
72
) and (
74
).

4.2.1
The case
d\geq p

Our first result corresponds to an adaptation of
Section

4.1.1
to
the case where
\varphi=\|F\|^{k}
. Indeed, in this case we have that

\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)=0
and we can tighten our previous results.

Proposition 15
.

Assume
1
,
2
and that
d\geq p
. Then
there exists
\eta>0
with

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\|F\|^{k})/\varepsilon=\mathcal{C}_{k}\mathcal{I}_{0}(1)
, where for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi\mathbbm{1}_{F^{-1}(\operatorname{B}(0,\eta))})
for any
\varphi\in\mathrm{C}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})

and

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{k}=\left.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\|t\|^{k}\exp[-\|t\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}t\middle/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\exp[-\|t\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}t\right.}\;.

(115)

Proof.

Let
\eta>0
be given by
Section

4.1.1
with
\varphi=1
. We recall
that for any
\varepsilon>0
we have that

\textstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\|F\|^{k})=\int_{F^{-1}(\operatorname{B}(0,\eta))}\|F(x)\|^{k}\Psi(x)\exp[-\|F(x)\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x/C_{\varepsilon}\;.}

(116)

Using the coarea formula, see
Theorem

C.6
, we have for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\textstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\|F\|^{k})/\varepsilon=\int_{\operatorname{B}(0,\eta)}(\left\|t\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon)\exp[-\left\|t\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathcal{L}_{t}(1)\mathrm{d}t/C_{\varepsilon}\;,}

(117)

where
\mathcal{L}_{t}(1)
is defined in (
75
) for any
t\in\operatorname{B}(0,\eta)
. Using the change of variable
t\mapsto\varepsilon^{1/k}t
we
have for any
\varepsilon>0

\textstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\|F\|^{k})/\varepsilon=\int_{\operatorname{B}(0,\eta/\varepsilon^{1/k})}\left\|t\right\|^{k}\exp[-\left\|t\right\|^{k}]\mathcal{L}_{t\varepsilon^{1/k}}(1)\mathrm{d}t/C_{1}\;.}

(118)

For any
\varepsilon>0
let
g_{\varepsilon}:\ \mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for any

t\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

g_{\varepsilon}(t)=\mathcal{L}_{t\varepsilon^{1/k}}(1)\mathbbm{1}_{\operatorname{B}(0,\eta/\varepsilon^{1/k})}(t)
. Note that for any
t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

\varepsilon>0
,
\left|g_{\varepsilon}(t)\right|\leq\sup_{\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,\eta)}\left|\mathcal{L}_{t}(1)\right|
. In addition, we have that for any

t\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}g_{\varepsilon}(t)=\mathcal{L}_{0}(1)
using

Section

4.1.1
. Therefore, we get that

\textstyle{\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\|F\|^{k})/\varepsilon=\mathcal{L}_{0}(1)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\|t\right\|^{k}\exp[-\left\|t\right\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}t/C_{1}\;.}

(119)

We conclude the proof upon noting that
\mathcal{I}_{0}(1)=\mathcal{L}_{0}(1)
and that
\mathcal{C}_{k}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\left\|t\right\|^{k}\exp[-\left\|t\right\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}t/C_{1}
.
∎

4.2.2
The case
d\leq p

We now adapt
Section

4.1.2
to the case where

\varphi=\|F\|^{k}
.

Proposition 16
.

Assume
1
,
2
and
d\leq p
. Let

\mathsf{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
open and bounded such that
F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{U}
.
Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\|F\|^{k})/\varepsilon=\mathcal{C}_{k}\mathcal{I}_{0}(1)
, where for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{V}})
for any

\varphi\in\mathrm{C}(\bar{\mathsf{U}},\mathbb{R})
, with
\mathsf{V}
open such that

F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{V}\subset\mathsf{U}
, and

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{k}=\left.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\|t\|^{k}\exp[-\|t\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}t\middle/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\exp[-\|t\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}t\right.\;.}

(120)

Proof.

Let
\{x_{0}^{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{N}
,
\{\mathsf{W}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{N}
and
\{\Phi_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{N}
be given as in the proof of
Section

4.1.2
.
Let

\mathsf{V}=\cup_{\ell=1}^{N}\Phi_{\ell}(\Omega_{\ell})
. Using for any

\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
the change of variable
x\mapsto\Phi_{\ell}(x)
and

x\mapsto\varepsilon^{1/k}x
we have

\textstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\|F\|^{k})\varepsilon=\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\int_{\Omega_{\ell}/\varepsilon^{1/k}}\|x\|^{k}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}]\Psi(\Phi_{\ell}(\varepsilon^{1/k}x))\det(\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\ell}(\varepsilon^{1/k}x))\mathrm{d}x/C_{1}\;.}

(121)

For any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and
\varepsilon>0
, let

g_{\ell,\varepsilon}:\ \mathbb{R}^{p}\to\mathbb{R}
such that for any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,
\varepsilon>0
and

x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
we have

g_{\ell,\varepsilon}(x)=\Psi(\Phi_{\ell}(\varepsilon^{1/k}x))\det(\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\ell}(\varepsilon^{1/k}x))\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{\ell}/\varepsilon^{1/k}}(x)\;.

(122)

Note that for any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,
\varepsilon>0
and

x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
we have

|g_{\ell,\varepsilon}(x)|\leq\sup\{\left|\Psi(\Phi_{\ell}(x))\right|\left|\det(\mathrm{D}\Phi_{\ell}(x))\right|\,:\;\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\},\ \ x\in\Omega_{\ell}\}\;.

(123)

In addition, we have that for any
\ell\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and

x\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
,

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}g_{\ell,\varepsilon}(x)=\Psi(x_{0}^{\ell})\mathrm{J}F(x_{0}^{\ell})^{-1}
.
We conclude upon using the dominated convergence theorem.
∎

4.2.3
Main result

Proposition 17
.

Assume
1
and
2
. Let

\mathsf{U}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
open and bounded such that
F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{U}
.
Then

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}(\|F\|^{k})/\varepsilon=\mathcal{C}_{k}
,
where

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{k}=\left.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\|t\|^{k}\exp[-\|t\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}t\middle/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\exp[-\|t\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}t\right.\;.}

(124)

Proof.

Let
\eta>0
be given by
Section

4.1.1
with
\varphi=1
and let

\mathsf{V}=F^{-1}(\operatorname{B}(0,\eta))
if
d\geq p
. If
d\leq p
, let
\mathsf{V}
be
given by
Section

4.2.2
. We have that

F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{V}
. For any
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right)
we define

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{out}}(\|F\|^{k})=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\|F\|^{k}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{c}}})
and

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{in}}(\|F\|^{k})=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\|F\|^{k}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{V}})
. Using
Lemma

A.3
, we have that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{out}}(\|F\|^{k})/\varepsilon=0
. Hence, using
Section

4.2.1
if
d\geq p
and

Section

4.2.2
if
d\leq p
, we get that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\|F\|^{k})/\varepsilon=\mathcal{C}_{k}\mathcal{I}_{0}(1)
. Similarly, using
Lemma

A.3
and

Section

4.1.1
we have that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(1)=\mathcal{I}_{0}(1)
, which concludes the proof upon
remarking that for any
\varepsilon>0
,

\pi_{\varepsilon}^{\Psi}(\|F\|^{k})=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\|F\|^{k})/\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(1)
.
∎

We are now ready to prove a generalization of
Section

3.1.2
.

Proposition 18
.

Let
\mu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,
F:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
. Assume that the conditions of
Section

3.1.1
with

G_{\varepsilon}=\|F\|^{k}-\varepsilon
for any
\varepsilon>0
are satisfied
and for any
\varepsilon>0
, let
\rho_{\varepsilon}
the macrocanonical distribution
with constraint
G_{\varepsilon}
and reference measure
\mu
. Assume that there
exists
\Psi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
\mu
admits a density
w.r.t. the Lebesgue measure given by
\Psi
. In addition, assume that

1
and
2
hold. Then, we have that

\theta_{\varepsilon}\sim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{C}_{k}/\varepsilon
, where

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}_{k}=\left.\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\|t\|^{k}\exp[-\|t\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}t\middle/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{p}}\exp[-\|t\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}t\right.\;.}

(125)

Proof.

Recall that using
Section

3.1.1
we have that for any
\varepsilon>0

and
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\textstyle{\rho_{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{A})=\left.\int_{\mathsf{A}}\Psi(x)\exp[-\theta_{\varepsilon}\|F(x)\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}x\middle/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Psi(x)\exp[-\theta_{\varepsilon}\|F(x)\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}x\right.\;.}

(126)

Hence, using
Section

4.2.3
we have that

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\rho_{\varepsilon}(\|F\|^{k})\theta_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{C}_{k}
. Since
\rho_{\varepsilon}[G_{\varepsilon}]=0
we have also have that

\rho_{\varepsilon}[\|F\|^{k}]=\varepsilon
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Note that
Section

3.1.2
is obtained upon noting that
\mathcal{C}_{2}=p/2
.

4.3
Proofs of
Section

3.2

In
Section

4.3.1
, we establish

Section

3.2.2
. In
Section

4.3.2
we use stability results from

[
60
,
8
]
to obtain
Section

3.2.2
.
Finally, we prove
Section

3.2.2
in
Section

4.3.3
. Additional
technical results are postponed to
Appendix

B
.

4.3.1
Proof of
Section

3.2.2

In this section, we prove
Section

3.2.2
which is an application
of a parametric version of the results presented in
Section

4.1.2
. We
refer to
Appendix

B
for a detailed presentation of these
results. We will apply them in the context of the non-convex
minimization setting presented in
Section

3.2
which we recall here.

We aim at minimizing
U:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
. We assume that there exist a
topological space
(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{B}(\mathsf{Z}))
, a probability measure

\mu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathsf{Z},\mathcal{Z})
and
u:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{Z}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
such that
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{U(x)=\int_{\mathsf{Z}}u(x,z)\mathrm{d}\mu(z)\;.}

(127)

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we define
U_{n}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathsf{Z}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
such
that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
z^{1:n}=\{z_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}

\textstyle{U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})=(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}u(x,z_{i})\;.}

(128)

For all
\varepsilon>0
, when it is well-defined we denote

\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}:\ \mathsf{Z}^{n}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\left[0,1\right]
the Markov
kernel such that for any
z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
and
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we
have

\textstyle{\updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}(\mathsf{A})=\left.\int_{\mathsf{A}}\exp[-U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\middle/\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\right.\;.}

(129)

Similarly, when it is well-defined, we denote
\mathrm{S}_{0}:\ \mathsf{Z}^{n}\times\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\left[0,1\right]
the Markov
kernel such that for any
z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
and
\mathsf{A}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we
have

\textstyle{\updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{S}_{0}(\mathsf{A})=\left.\int_{\mathsf{A}\cap\mathsf{C}_{n}(z^{1:n})}\det(\nabla_{x}^{2}U_{n}(x,z^{1:n}))^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{0}(x)\middle/\int_{\mathsf{C}_{n}(z^{1:n})}\det(\nabla_{x}^{2}U_{n}(x,z^{1:n}))^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{0}(x)\right.\;,}

(130)

where
\mathsf{C}_{n}(z^{1:n})=\operatorname*{arg\,min}U_{n}(\cdot,z^{1:n})
. We recall that for any

\upbeta>0
,
\sigma_{\upbeta}^{\star}
is defined in
(
49
). We begin with the following proposition.

Proposition 19
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and assume
3
(n)
and

4
(n)
. Then there exist
C\geq 0
and

\upbeta,\bar{\varepsilon}>0
such that for any
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right)

\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\mathbf{W}_{1}(\updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}^{n},\updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{S}_{0}^{n})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})\leq C(1+D_{n})(\varepsilon^{1/2}+\varepsilon^{-d/2}\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{1:n}}\exp[-c^{\star}(z^{1:n})/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n}))\;,}

(131)

with

D_{n}=\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\sigma_{\upbeta}^{\star}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})<+\infty
and
C,\bar{\varepsilon},\upbeta
independent from
n
.

Proof.

The proof of this result is a direct application
of
Proposition

B.5
which is a parametric version of

Theorem

3
. In order to apply

Proposition

B.5
, we check that
5
is
satisfied for
\mathsf{Z}\leftarrow\mathsf{Z}^{n}
and
u\leftarrow U_{n}
. We only need to
check that there exists
\mathtt{m}_{0},\upalpha_{0}>0
and
R_{0}
such that for any

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
\|x\|\geq R_{0}
and
z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
,

U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})\geq\mathtt{m}_{0}\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha_{0}}
. We have that for any

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
z\in\mathsf{Z}

\displaystyle\textstyle{u(x,z)=u(0,z)+\int_{0}^{1}\langle\nabla_{x}u(tx,z),x\rangle\mathrm{d}t\geq-A-\mathtt{c}+(\mathtt{m}/2)\|x\|^{2}\;.}

(132)

Hence, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
with

\|x\|\geq 2((A+\mathtt{c})/\mathtt{m})^{1/2}
we have that for any

z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
,
U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})\geq(\mathtt{m}/4)\|x\|^{2}
. Hence,
combining this result,
3
(n)
and

4
(n)
, we can apply

Proposition

B.5
which states that for any
\varphi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}

M_{1,\varphi}
-Lipschitz function with
M_{1,\varphi},C_{\varphi}\geq 0
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left|\varphi(x)\right|\leq C_{\varphi}\exp[C_{\varphi}\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha}]
then,
there exist
B_{2}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
and
\upbeta>0
such that

\left|\updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}[\varphi]-\updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{S}_{0}[\varphi]\right|\leq B_{2}(C_{\varphi})(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+\sigma_{\upbeta}^{\star}(z^{1:n}))\{\varepsilon^{1/2}+\varepsilon^{-d/2}\exp[-c^{\star}(z^{1:n})/\varepsilon]\}\;,

(133)

with

M_{0,\varphi}=\sup\{|\varphi(x)|\,:\;x\in\mathsf{K}\}
,

\mathsf{K}
,
B_{2}
and
\upbeta
independent from
z
and
B_{2}
non-decreasing.
The rest of the proof is similar to the one of
Section

2
upon
replacing
Theorem

1
by (
133
).
∎

The proof of
Section

3.2.2
is then a direct application of

[
60
, Proposition 3.3]
and
Section

4.3.1
.

4.3.2
Proof of
Section

3.2.2

In this section, we prove
Section

3.2.2
. We start by recalling a
proposition from
[
60
, Proposition 3.5]
about the uniform
stability of the exponential measure with potential
U_{n}
, see
[
8
]

for a definition of the uniform stability.

Lemma 20
.

Assume that
3
(n)
holds uniformly w.r.t
n
. Then
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\varepsilon>0
,
z_{0}^{1:n},z_{1}^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
which
only differs along one index, we have

\textstyle{|\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{0}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}u(\cdot,z)-\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{1}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}u(\cdot,z)|\leq 4(\mathtt{M}^{2}(\mathtt{c}+d\varepsilon)/\mathtt{m}+B^{2})\mathtt{c_{LS}}/(n\varepsilon)\;,}

(134)

where
\mathtt{c_{LS}}\geq 0
is such that for any
z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
,

\updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}
satisfies the logarithmic Sobolev
inequality with constant
\mathtt{c_{LS}}
, see
[
60
, Proposition 3.2]
.

For completeness, we recall that a probability measure
\nu\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

is said to satisfy the logarithmic Sobolev inequality with constant
\mathtt{c_{LS}}
if
for any
\pi\in\mathscr{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with positive density w.r.t
\nu
given by

g\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R})
we have

\textstyle{\operatorname{KL}\left(\pi|\nu\right)\leq 2\mathtt{c_{LS}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|\nabla\log(g(x))\|^{2}\mathrm{d}\pi(x)\;.}

(135)

We are now ready to show that the limiting
measures are stable.

Proposition 21
.

Assume that
3
(n)
and

4
(n)
hold uniformly w.r.t
n\in\mathbb{N}
. In addition, assume that there exist

C_{0},C_{\sigma},\alpha,\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}>0
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\varepsilon\in(0,\bar{\varepsilon}_{0}]

\textstyle{\varepsilon^{-d/2}\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\exp[-c^{\star}(z^{1:n})/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})\leq C_{0}\varepsilon^{\alpha}\;,}\qquad\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\sigma_{\upbeta}^{\star}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})\leq C_{\sigma}\;.}

(136)

Then there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
\eta\in\left(0,1\right)
,
there exists
C\geq 0
such that for any
z\in\mathsf{Z}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
with

n\geq n_{0}
and
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, we have

\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n+1}}|\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{0}}\mathrm{S}_{0}u(\cdot,z)-\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{1}}\mathrm{S}_{0}u(\cdot,z)|\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z_{0}^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu(z_{1,j})\leq Cn^{-\eta s/(1+\eta s)}\;,}

(137)

where
s=\min(\alpha/2,1/4)
and for any
z_{0}^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
we let

z_{1}^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
with
z_{0,i}=z_{1,i}
for any

i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
such that
i\neq j
.

Proof.

Let
\eta\in\left(0,1\right)
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq n_{0}
and

n_{0}^{-1/(1+\eta s)}<\bar{\varepsilon}=\min(\bar{\varepsilon}_{0},\bar{\varepsilon}_{1})
(where

\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}
is given by
\bar{\varepsilon}_{1}\leftarrow\bar{\varepsilon}
in

Section

4.3.1
) with
s=\min(\alpha/2,1/4)
,

\varepsilon\in\left(0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right)
and

z_{0}^{1:n},z_{1}^{1:n},z\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
where there exists

j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that for any
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
i\neq j
,

z_{0,i}=z_{1,i}
. Using the triangle inequality we have for any

\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle|\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{0}}\mathrm{S}_{0}u(\cdot,z)-\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{1}}\mathrm{S}_{0}u(\cdot,z)|\leq|\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{0}}\mathrm{S}_{0}u(\cdot,z)-\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{0}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}u(\cdot,z)|

(138)

\displaystyle\qquad+|\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{0}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}u(\cdot,z)-\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{1}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}u(\cdot,z)|+|\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{1}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}u(\cdot,z)-\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{1}}\mathrm{S}_{0}u(\cdot,z)|\;.

(139)

Using
[
60
, Lemma 3.5]
and
Lemma

B.6
, there
exists
C_{0}\geq 0
(independent from
\varepsilon,z^{1:n}_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}

and
n\in\mathbb{N}
) such that for any
i\in\{0,1\}

|\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{i}}\mathrm{S}_{0}u(\cdot,z)-\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{i}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}u(\cdot,z)|\leq C_{0}\mathbf{W}_{2}(\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{i}}\mathrm{S}_{0},\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{i}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon})\;.

(140)

Using this result,
Lemma

B.6
and

Lemma

B.7
, there exists
C_{1}\geq 0
(independent from

\varepsilon,z^{1:n}_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
) such that for
any
i\in\{0,1\}

|\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{i}}\mathrm{S}_{0}u(\cdot,z)-\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{i}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}u(\cdot,z)|\leq C_{1}\mathbf{W}_{1}^{\eta/2}(\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{i}}\mathrm{S}_{0},\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{i}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon})\;.

(141)

In addition, using
Section

4.3.1
, there exists
C_{2}\geq 0
(independent from

\varepsilon,z^{1:n}_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
) such that for
any
i\in\{0,1\}

\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\mathbf{W}_{1}(\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{i}}\mathrm{S}_{0},\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{i}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z_{i}^{1:n})\leq C_{2}\max(\varepsilon^{\alpha},\varepsilon^{1/2})\;.}

(142)

Combining this result, (
141
) and the fact that

t\mapsto t^{\eta/2}
is concave, we get that there exists
C_{3}\geq 0

(independent from
\varepsilon,z^{1:n}_{i}
for
i\in\{0,1\}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
)
such that for any
i\in\{0,1\}

\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}|\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{i}}\mathrm{S}_{0}u(\cdot,z)-\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{i}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}u(\cdot,z)|\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z_{i}^{1:n})\leq C_{3}\max(\varepsilon^{\eta\alpha/2},\varepsilon^{\eta/4})\;.}

(143)

In addition, using
Section

4.3.2
we have

\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n+1}}|\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{0}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}u(\cdot,z)-\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{1}}\mathrm{S}_{\varepsilon}u(\cdot,z)|\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes}(z^{1:n}_{0})\mathrm{d}\mu(z_{1,j})\leq 4(\mathtt{M}^{2}(\mathtt{c}+d\varepsilon)/\mathtt{m}+B^{2})\mathtt{c_{LS}}/(n\varepsilon)\;,}

(144)

Combining this result, (
138
) and (
143
) we get

\displaystyle\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n+1}}|\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{0}}\mathrm{S}_{0}u(\cdot,z)-\updelta_{z^{1:n}_{1}}\mathrm{S}_{0}u(\cdot,z)|\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes}(z^{1:n}_{0})\mathrm{d}\mu(z_{1,j})}

(145)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq 4(\mathtt{M}^{2}(\mathtt{c}+d\bar{\varepsilon})/\mathtt{m}+B^{2})\mathtt{c_{LS}}/(n\varepsilon)+2C_{3}\max(\varepsilon^{\eta\alpha/2},\varepsilon^{\eta/4})

(146)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq 4(\mathtt{M}^{2}(\mathtt{c}+d\bar{\varepsilon})/\mathtt{m}+B^{2})\mathtt{c_{LS}}/(n\varepsilon)+2C_{3}\varepsilon^{\eta s}\;.

(147)

with
s=\min(\alpha/2,1/4)
. We conclude the proof upon letting

\varepsilon=n^{-1/(1+\eta s)}

∎

The stability of the limiting measures allows us to establish

Section

3.2.2
which provides quantitative bounds on

\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U]-U^{\star}
for large values of
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Indeed, once
Section

4.3.2
is established the proof of

Section

3.2.2
is classical and follows the lines of
[
60
, Section
3.7]
.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
n\geq n_{0}
with
n_{0}
given by

Section

3.2.2
. For any
z^{1:n}\in\mathsf{Z}^{n}
we define

U_{n}^{\star}(z^{1:n})=\inf\{U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
. Using that

U^{\star}\geq\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}U_{n}^{\star}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})
we have

\displaystyle\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U]-U^{\star}\leq\textstyle{\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U]-\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}U_{n}^{\star}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})}

(148)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U]-\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})\mathrm{S}_{0}(z^{1:n},\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})}

(149)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}U_{n}(x,\tilde{z}^{1:n})\mathrm{S}_{0}(z^{1:n},\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(\tilde{z}^{1:n})}

(150)

\displaystyle\qquad\textstyle{-\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})\mathrm{S}_{0}(z^{1:n},\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})}

(151)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\{u(x,\tilde{z}_{i})-u(x,z_{i})\}\mathrm{S}_{0}(z^{1:n},\mathrm{d}x)\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu(\tilde{z}_{i})}

(152)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{\mathsf{Z}}\int_{\mathsf{Z}^{n}}\{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(x,z_{i})\mathrm{S}_{0}(z^{1:n},\mathrm{d}x)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}u(x,z_{i})\mathrm{S}_{0}(\tilde{z}_{i}^{1:n},\mathrm{d}x)\}\}\mathrm{d}\mu^{\otimes n}(z^{1:n})\mathrm{d}\mu(\tilde{z}_{i})\;,}

(153)

where for any
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, we have that for any

j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
\tilde{z}_{i,j}=z_{i}
and

\tilde{z}_{i,i}=\tilde{z}_{i}
. Using
Section

3.2.2
we have

\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}U-U^{\star}\leq C_{\eta}n^{-s\eta/(1+\eta s)}
with

C_{\eta}\geq 0
, which concludes the proof.

4.3.3
Proof of
Section

3.2.2

We recall that
u
is given in (
58
). We divide the proof
into two parts.

(a)

First, we prove that

\textstyle{\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}(\left(-\uppi/2,\uppi/2\right))-\pi_{0}(\left(-\uppi/2,\uppi/2\right))=-1/2}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Let

n\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that
z<0
. Then the minimum of
x\mapsto u(x,z)

is attained on
\left[\uppi,+\infty\right)
. Denote

\bar{u}:\ \mathbb{R}\times\left[-1/2,0\right)
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}

and
z\in\left[-1/2,0\right)
,

\bar{u}(x,z)=h(x)+xz+\uppi z+1-\cos(3x)
with

h(x)=x^{4}/(1+x^{2})
. Note that for any
x\geq 0
and

z\in\left[-1/2,0\right)
,
\bar{u}(x,z)=u(x+\uppi,z)
. There exists

a\in\left[0,\uppi/3\right]
such that for any
x\geq 0
,
h^{\prime}(x)-1\leq 0

if
x\leq a
and
h^{\prime}(x)-1>0
otherwise. Hence, we get that for any

x\in\mathbb{R}
with
x\geq\uppi/3
and
z\in\left[-1/2,0\right)

\textstyle{\bar{u}(x,z)-\bar{u}(0,z)=\bar{u}(x,z)-\uppi z\geq h(\uppi/3)-\uppi/6>0\;.}

(154)

Therefore, for any
z\in\left[-1/2,0\right)
, the global minimum of

x\mapsto u(x,z)
is attained on
\left(\uppi,4\uppi/3\right)
. We have that for
any
x\in\left[\uppi/6,\uppi/3\right]
and
z\in\left[-1/2,0\right)

\textstyle{\partial_{1}\bar{u}(x,z)\geq h^{\prime}(x)-1/2+3(2-(6/\uppi)x)>0\;.}

(155)

In addition, we have that for any
z\in\left[-1/2,0\right)
,

\partial_{1}\bar{u}(0,z)=-z
. Hence, there exists

\bar{x}(z)\in\left[0,\uppi/6\right]
such that

\partial_{1}\bar{u}(\bar{x}(z),z)=0
. In addition we have that for any

z\in\left[-1/2,0\right)
,
x\mapsto\partial_{1}\bar{u}(x,z)
is increasing on

\left[0,\uppi/6\right]
. Therefore, for any
z\in\left[-1/2,0\right)
there
exists a unique minimizer of
x\mapsto u(x,z)
on

\left[\uppi,5\uppi/6\right]
given by
x^{\star}(z)=\uppi+\bar{x}(z)
. The
same conclusion holds with
x^{\star}(z)\in\left[-5\uppi/6,-\uppi\right]
if

z\in\left(0,1/2\right]
. We have that

\lim_{z\to 0}\sup\{\|u(x,z)-u(x,0)\|\,:\;x\in\left[-5\uppi/6,5\uppi/6\right]\}=0
. Therefore we have that every limit
point of
\{x^{\star}(z)\}_{z<0}
when
z\to 0
is a global minimizer
of
u(\cdot,0)
. But recall that

\{x^{\star}(z)\}_{z<0}\subset\left[\uppi,5\uppi/6\right]
. Therefore,
every limit point of
\{x^{\star}(z)\}_{z<0}
is equal to
\uppi
and we
have that
\lim_{z\to 0,z>0}x^{\star}(z)=\uppi
. For any

n\in\mathbb{N}
, denote
g_{n}
the density of
T_{\#}\mu^{\otimes n}

where
T:\ \mathsf{Z}^{n}\to\mathsf{Z}
is given by

T(z^{1:n})=(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}
. For any
r,\varepsilon>0
there
exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with

n\geq n_{0}
,

\int_{\operatorname{B}(0,r)^{\mathrm{c}}}g_{n}(z)\mathrm{d}z\leq\varepsilon
. Let

\varphi\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R},\mathbb{R})
bounded and
\varepsilon>0
. Let
r>0
such that for any

z\in[0,r]
,

|\varphi(x^{\star}(z))-\varphi(-\uppi)|\leq\varepsilon
and for
any
z\in[-r,0]
,

|\varphi(x^{\star}(z))-\varphi(\uppi)|\leq\varepsilon
. Using
this result, we have for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n\geq n_{0}

\displaystyle\textstyle{|\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[\varphi]-(\varphi(-\uppi)+\varphi(\uppi))/2|}

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{\int_{0}^{+\infty}|\varphi(x^{\star}(z))-\varphi(-\uppi)|g_{n}(z)\mathrm{d}z+\int_{-\infty}^{0}|\varphi(x^{\star}(z))-\varphi(\uppi)|g_{n}(z)\mathrm{d}z}

(156)

\displaystyle\leq(1+2\|\varphi\|_{\infty})\varepsilon\;.

(157)

Therefore, we get that

\lim_{n\to+\infty}\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[\varphi]=(\varphi(-\uppi)+\varphi(\uppi))/2
, which concludes the first part
of the proof.

(b)

Second, we prove that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U]-U^{\star}\leq(\pi/(6\sqrt{3}))n^{-1/2}
. Note that for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and

z^{1:n}\in\left[-1/2,1/2\right]^{n}
, with
\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}\neq 0
,

\updelta_{z^{1:n}}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U]=U(x^{\star}(\bar{z}^{1:n}))
with

x^{\star}(\bar{z}^{1:n})\in\left[-5\uppi/6,5\uppi/6\right]
. We also have that for
any
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
z_{1},z_{2}\in\left[-1/2,1/2\right]
,

\textstyle{|u(x,z_{1})-u(x,z_{2})|\leq|x||z_{1}-z_{2}|}
. In particular, we have that for
any
z^{1:n}\in\left[-1/2,1/2\right]^{n}
and

x\in\left[-\uppi/3,\uppi/3\right]

\textstyle{|U(x)-U_{n}(x,z^{1:n})|\leq(\uppi/3)|(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}|\;.}

(158)

Hence, using this result and that
U^{\star}=U(\uppi/3)=0
, we have that for any
z^{1:n}\in\left[-1/2,1/2\right]

\textstyle{U_{n}(x^{\star}(\bar{z}^{1:n}),z^{1:n})\leq U_{n}(\uppi/3,z^{1:n})\leq U^{\star}+(\pi/3)|(1/n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}z_{i}|\;.}

(159)

Combining this result and that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}z^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(z)=1/12
we have
\mu^{\otimes n}\mathrm{S}_{0}[U]-U^{\star}\leq(\pi/(6\sqrt{3}))n^{-1/2}
,
which concludes the proof.

∎

Organization of the appendix

In this supplementary material we derive technical lemmas and additional
results. In particular, we gather the technical lemmas of

Section

2
in
Appendix

A
and the ones of

Section

4.3.1
in
Appendix

B
. In

Appendix

C
, we recall basic results from differential geometry
and geometric measure theory.

Appendix A
Technical results for
Section

2

In this section, we derive some technical lemmas used in

Section

4.1
in order to prove
Theorem

3
and
other results from
Section

2
. We recall that for any

\varphi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
\varepsilon>0
, when this is well-defined,
we set

\displaystyle\textstyle{\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi)=C_{\varepsilon}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x)\Psi(x)\exp[-\left\|F(x)\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x\;,\quad\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(1)\;,}

(160)

\displaystyle\textstyle{C_{\varepsilon}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x=\varepsilon^{d/k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}x=\varepsilon^{d/k}C_{1}\;.}

(161)

In addition, we define

\textstyle{\mathcal{I}_{0}(\varphi)=\int_{F^{-1}(0)}\varphi(x)\Psi(x)\mathrm{J}F(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-\hat{d}}(x)\;,\quad\mathcal{J}_{0}=\mathcal{I}_{0}(1)\;,}

(162)

with
\hat{d}=\min(d,p)
.

In
Section

A.1
we establish a link between a Hessian
computed on the normal bundle of a manifold and the generalized Jacobian. In

Section

A.2
we derive technical truncation bounds for the
proof of
Theorem

3
. Explicit controls of some derivative
are presented in
Section

A.3
in order to derive

Section

4.1.1
. Finally, we present a smoothing lemma in

Section

A.4
which is key to weaken the regularity
assumptions of
Section

4.1.3
.

A.1
From normal Hessian to generalized Jacobian

Let
f\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathrm{M}
a manifold in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For any

x\in\mathrm{M}
we define
\nabla_{\perp}^{2}f(x)
to be the projection
of the Hessian on the orthogonal of the tangent space of
\mathrm{M}
at
x
,
see
[
44
]
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
U:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
F\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
such
that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
U(x)=\|F(x)\|^{2}
. In addition,
assume that
F^{-1}(0)\neq\emptyset
and that for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
,

\mathrm{J}F(x)>0
. Then,
\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{U(x)\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
is a smooth
manifold and for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
we have that

\det(\nabla^{2}_{\perp}U(x))=\mathrm{J}F(x)^{2}
.

Proof.

First, we have that

\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{U(x)\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}=F^{-1}(0)
. Hence,

\operatorname*{arg\,min}\{U(x)\,:\;x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\}
is a smooth manifold since

F\in\mathrm{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
. Let
x\in F^{-1}(0)
. We have that

\nabla^{2}U(x)=\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top}\mathrm{D}F(x)
. Note that

\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top}=(\nabla F_{1}(x),\dots,\nabla F_{p}(x))
is a basis of

\mathrm{ker}(\mathrm{D}F(x))^{\perp}
, where we recall that

\mathrm{ker}(\mathrm{D}F(x))
is the tangent space to
F^{-1}(0)
at
x
. Denote
by
O(x)=(f_{1}(x),\dots,f_{p}(x))
the orthonormal basis of

\mathrm{ker}(\mathrm{D}F(x))^{\perp}
obtained from
\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top}
using the
Gram-Schmidt process. There exists a triangular
p\times p
matrix
T(x)

such that
O(x)=\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top}T(x)
. We also have

\operatorname{Id}=O(x)^{\top}O(x)=T(x)^{\top}\mathrm{D}F(x)\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top}T(x)\;.

(163)

Hence, we get that
\det(T(x))=\mathrm{J}F(x)^{-1}
. We also have that

\det(\nabla_{\perp}^{2}U(x))=\det(O(x)^{\top}\nabla^{2}U(x)O(x))=\det(T(x)^{\top}\mathrm{D}F(x)\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top}\mathrm{D}F(x)\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top}T(x))=\mathrm{J}F(x)^{2}\;,

(164)

which concludes the proof.
∎

A.2
Truncation and lower bounds

Lemma A.2
.

Assume
1
and
2
. Then, for any

\bar{\varepsilon}\geq 0
there exists
A_{0}\geq 0
such that for any

\varepsilon\in\left[0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right]
,
\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}\geq A_{0}m_{0,\Psi}
with

m_{0,\Psi}=\inf\{\Psi(x)\,:\;x\in F^{-1}(0)\}
and
A_{0}

independent from
\Psi
.

Proof.

Since
F(0)=0
and
F\in\mathrm{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
, there exists
M\geq 0

such that for any
x\in\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,1)
,
\left\|F(x)\right\|\leq M\left\|x\right\|
. Note
that
F^{-1}(0)
is compact since

\lim_{\left\|x\right\|\to+\infty}\left\|F(x)\right\|=+\infty
and

F\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
. Hence, since for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
,

\Psi(x)>0
and
\Psi\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}_{+})
there exists

\eta\in\left(0,1\right)
such that for any

x\in\operatorname{B}(0,\eta)\cup F^{-1}(0)
,
\Psi(x)\geq m_{0,\Psi}/2
. Using this result we have for any
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\textstyle{\varepsilon^{-d/k}C_{1}^{-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Psi(x)\exp[-\left\|F(x)\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x}

(165)

\displaystyle\geq\textstyle{\varepsilon^{-d/k}(m_{0,\Psi}/2)C_{1}^{-1}\int_{\operatorname{B}(0,\eta)}\exp[-M^{k}\left\|x\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x}

(166)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\geq C_{1}^{-1}(m_{0,\Psi}/2)M^{-d}\int_{\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,M\eta/\bar{\varepsilon}^{1/k})}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}x\;.}

(167)

Using that
F^{-1}(0)
is compact, there exists
\mathtt{M}\geq 0
such that for
any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
,
\mathrm{J}F(x)\leq\mathtt{M}
. Therefore, we get that

\textstyle{\mathcal{J}_{0}=\int_{F^{-1}(0)}\Psi(x)\mathrm{J}F(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{d-\hat{d}}(x)\geq m_{0,\Psi}\mathtt{M}^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{d-\hat{d}}(F^{-1}(0))\;.}

(168)

Since
\mathcal{H}^{d-\hat{d}}(F^{-1}(0))<+\infty
using

Lemma

C.5
in the case where
d\geq p
and the fact
that
\mathcal{H}^{0}(F^{-1}(0))<+\infty
if
d\leq p
(see the first part of the
proof of
Lemma

A.4
), we have that for any

\varepsilon\in\left[0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right]

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\varepsilon}\geq A_{0}m_{0,\Psi}\;,\qquad A_{0}=\min(A_{0}^{1},A_{0}^{2})\;,

(169)

\displaystyle\textstyle{A_{0}^{1}=(1/2)C_{1}^{-1}M^{-d}\int_{\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,M\eta/\bar{\varepsilon}^{1/k})}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{k}]\mathrm{d}x\;,}\quad A_{0}^{2}=M^{-1}\mathcal{H}^{d-\hat{d}}(F^{-1}(0))\;,

(170)

which concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma A.3
.

Assume
1
and
2
. Let

\varphi:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
C_{\varphi}\geq 0
such that for any

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\textstyle{|\varphi(x)|\leq C_{\varphi}\exp[C_{\varphi}\|x\|^{\upalpha k}]\;.}

(171)

Then, for any
\bar{\varepsilon}\in(0,\mathtt{m}^{k}/(1+C_{\varphi,\Psi}))
and

\mathsf{V}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
open and bounded such that
F^{-1}(0)\subset\mathsf{V}
there exist

\beta_{1}>0
and
A_{1}\in\mathrm{C}(\mathbb{R}_{+},\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that for any
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right)

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{out}}(\varphi)\leq A_{1}(C_{\varphi,\Psi})\varepsilon^{-d/k}\exp[-\beta_{1}/\varepsilon]\;,

(172)

with

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{out}}(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{c}}})
,

C_{\varphi,\Psi}=C_{\varphi}+C_{\Psi}+C_{\varphi}C_{\Psi}
and
A_{1},\beta_{1}

independent functions from
\varphi
and
\Psi
. Finally,
A_{1}
is
non-decreasing.

Proof.

First using
2
and (
171
) there exists

C_{\varphi,\Psi}
such that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\left|\varphi(x)\right|\Psi(x)\leq C_{\varphi,\Psi}\exp[C_{\varphi,\Psi}\left\|x\right\|]\;,\qquad C_{\varphi,\Psi}=C_{\varphi}+C_{\Psi}+C_{\varphi}C_{\Psi}\;.

(173)

Since
\mathsf{V}
is bounded
there exists
R^{\prime}\geq R
(where
R
is given in
1
) such that

\mathsf{V}\subset\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,R^{\prime})
. Note that for any
\varepsilon>0
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{\mathrm{out}}(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{1}(\varphi)+\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\varphi)\;,

(174)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{1}(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi\mathbbm{1}_{\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,R^{\prime})^{\mathrm{c}}})\;,\quad\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi\mathbbm{1}_{\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{c}}\cap\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,R^{\prime})})\;.

(175)

Let
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\bar{\varepsilon}\right)
, we divide the rest of the proof into two parts. First, we
bound
\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{1}(\varphi)
and then

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\varphi)
.

(a)

Let
u=(\mathtt{m}^{k}/\varepsilon-C_{\varphi,\Psi})^{1/\upalpha k}
(which
makes sense, since
\varepsilon<\mathtt{m}^{k}/(C_{\varphi,\Psi}+1)
). Since

R^{\prime}\geq R
we have using (
19
) and that
u\geq 1

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{1}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\textstyle{C_{1}^{-1}\varepsilon^{-d/k}\int_{\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,R^{\prime})^{\mathrm{c}}}\varphi(x)\Psi(x)\exp[-\left\|F(x)\right\|^{k}/\varepsilon]\mathrm{d}x}

(176)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{C_{1}^{-1}C_{\varphi,\Psi}\varepsilon^{-d/k}\int_{\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,R^{\prime})^{\mathrm{c}}}\exp[-(\mathtt{m}^{k}/\varepsilon-C_{\varphi,\Psi})\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha k}]\mathrm{d}x}

(177)

\displaystyle\leq\textstyle{C_{1}^{-1}C_{\varphi,\Psi}\varepsilon^{-d/k}\int_{\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,R^{\prime}u)^{\mathrm{c}}}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha k}]\mathrm{d}x\;.}

(178)

Let
C_{\upalpha}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha k}]\mathrm{d}x
. Using that
u=(\mathtt{m}^{k}/\varepsilon-C_{\varphi,\Psi})^{1/\upalpha k}
,
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{1}(\varphi)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\leq C_{1}^{-1}C_{\varphi,\Psi}\varepsilon^{-d/k}\int_{\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,R^{\prime}u)^{\mathrm{c}}}\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha k}]\mathrm{d}x}

(179)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\leq C_{1}^{-1}C_{\varphi,\Psi}\varepsilon^{-d/k}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp[\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha k}/2]\exp[-\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha k}]\mathrm{d}x\exp[-(R^{\prime}u)^{\upalpha k}/2]}

(180)

\displaystyle\leq 2^{d/\upalpha k}C_{\upalpha}C_{1}^{-1}C_{\varphi,\Psi}\exp[(R^{\prime})^{\upalpha k}C_{\varphi,\Psi}/2]\varepsilon^{-d/k}\exp[-(R^{\prime})^{\upalpha k}\mathtt{m}^{k}/(2\varepsilon)]\leq A_{1}^{1}\varepsilon^{-d/k}\exp[-\beta_{1}^{1}/\varepsilon]\;,

(181)

with

A_{1}^{1}=2^{d/\upalpha k}C_{\upalpha}C_{1}^{-1}C_{\varphi,\Psi}\exp[(R^{\prime})^{\upalpha k}C_{\varphi,\Psi}/2]\;,\qquad\beta_{1}^{1}=(R^{\prime})^{\upalpha k}\mathtt{m}^{k}/2\;.

(182)

(b)

Second, note that

\mathsf{K}=\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{c}}\cap\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,R^{\prime})
is
bounded and closed,
i.e.

\mathsf{K}
is compact. Note that for any

x\in\mathsf{K}
,
\left\|F(x)\right\|>0
, hence there exists
m>0
such that for
any
x\in\mathsf{K}
,
\left\|F(x)\right\|\geq m
. In addition, we have that for any

x\in\mathsf{K}
,

\left|\varphi(x)\right|\Psi(x)\leq C_{\varphi,\Psi}\exp[C_{\varphi,\Psi}\left\|x\right\|^{\upalpha k}]\leq C_{\varphi}\exp[C_{\varphi,\Psi}(R^{\prime})^{\upalpha k}]\;.

(183)

Therefore, we have

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\varphi)\leq C_{1}^{-1}C_{\varphi,\Psi}\varepsilon^{-d/k}\exp[C_{\varphi,\Psi}(R^{\prime})^{\upalpha k}]\exp[-m^{k}/\varepsilon]\lambda(\mathsf{K})\;,

(184)

where we recall that
\lambda(\mathsf{K})
is the Lebesgue measure of
\mathsf{K}
.
Since
\mathsf{K}\subset\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,R^{\prime})
we have

\mathcal{I}_{\varepsilon}^{2}(\varphi)\leq\uppi^{d/2}(R^{\prime})^{d}\Gamma^{-1}(d/2+1)C_{1}^{-1}C_{\varphi,\Psi}\exp[(R^{\prime})^{\upalpha k}]\varepsilon^{-d/k}\exp[-m^{k}/\varepsilon]\leq A_{1}^{2}\varepsilon^{-d/k}\exp[-\beta_{1}^{2}/\varepsilon]\;,

(185)

where
\Gamma:\ \left(0,+\infty\right)\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
is given for any

s\in\left(0,+\infty\right)
by

\Gamma(s)=\int_{0}^{+\infty}t^{s-1}\exp[-t]\mathrm{d}t
and

A_{1}^{2}=\uppi^{d/2}(R^{\prime})^{d}\Gamma^{-1}(d/2+1)C_{1}^{-1}C_{\varphi,\Psi}\exp[C_{\varphi,\Psi}(R^{\prime})^{\upalpha k}]\;,\qquad\beta_{1}^{2}=m^{k}\;.

(186)

We conclude the proof upon combining (
181
), (
185
),
letting
\beta_{1}=\min(\beta_{1}^{1},\beta_{1}^{2})
and
A_{1}=A_{1}^{1}+A_{1}^{2}
.
∎

Lemma A.4
.

Assume
1
and that
d\leq p
. Then there exist
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\{x_{0}^{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
and
\mathsf{W}_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
open such
that for any
k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,
x_{0}^{k}\in\mathsf{W}_{k}
,

F:\ \bar{\mathsf{W}}_{k}\to F(\bar{\mathsf{W}}_{k})
is a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism, for
any
x\in\mathsf{W}_{k}
,
\mathrm{d}F(x)
is injective and for any

j\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,
\bar{\mathsf{W}}_{k}\cap\bar{\mathsf{W}}_{j}=\emptyset
. In
addition,
F^{-1}(0)=\cup_{k=1}^{N}\{x_{0}^{k}\}
.

Proof.

Since, for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
,
\mathrm{J}F(x)>0
and
d\leq p
there exists

r_{x}>0
such that for any
y\in\bar{\operatorname{B}}(x,r_{x})
,
F(y)=F(x)
implies that

y=x
. Since
\lim_{\left\|x\right\|\to+\infty}\left\|F(x)\right\|=+\infty
we have that

F^{-1}(0)
is compact. Assume that
\mathcal{H}^{0}(F^{-1}(0))=+\infty
. Then,
there exists
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

F(x_{k})=0
and for any
j\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
,
x_{j}\neq x_{k}
. Up to
extraction, there exists
x^{\star}\in F^{-1}(0)
such that

\lim_{k\to+\infty}x_{k}=x^{\star}
and for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

x_{k}\neq x^{\star}
. Hence, there exists
k\in\mathbb{N}
such that

x_{k}\in\bar{\operatorname{B}}(0,r_{x^{\star}})
which is absurd. Hence

\mathcal{H}^{0}(F^{-1}(0))<+\infty
. In what follows, we let
N=\mathcal{H}^{0}(F^{-1}(0))

and denote
\{x_{0}^{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}\in(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
such that

F^{-1}(0)=\{x_{0}^{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}
. There exists

\{r_{k}^{\prime}\}_{k=1}^{N}\in(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{N}
such that for any

k,j\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,

\bar{\operatorname{B}}(x_{0}^{k},r_{k}^{\prime})\cap\bar{\operatorname{B}}(x_{0}^{j},r_{j}^{\prime})=\emptyset
. For any

k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, we let

\mathsf{V}_{k}=\operatorname{B}(x_{0}^{k},\min(r_{k}^{\prime},r_{x_{0}^{k}}/2))
. Let
k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
. By construction,
F:\ \bar{\mathsf{V}}_{k}\to F(\bar{\mathsf{V}}_{k})
is bijective
and continuous. Since
\bar{\mathsf{V}}_{k}
is compact, we have that

F:\ \bar{\mathsf{V}}_{k}\to F(\bar{\mathsf{V}}_{k})
is a homeomorphism. Since for any

k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,
\mathrm{J}F(x_{0}^{k})>0
, there exists
m>0
such that for
any
k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

v^{\top}H(x_{0}^{k},x_{0}^{k})v\geq m\|v\|^{2}
with

H(x,y)=\mathrm{D}F(x)^{\top}\mathrm{D}F(y)
for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. For any

k\in\{1,\dots,N\}
, there exists
\mathsf{W}_{k}\subset\mathsf{V}_{k}
such that for
any
x,y\in\mathsf{W}_{k}
we have

\|H(x,y)-H(x_{0}^{k},x_{0}^{k})\|_{2}\leq m/2
. Therefore we have for any

x,y\in\mathsf{W}_{k}

\displaystyle\left\|F(x)-F(y)\right\|^{2}=\textstyle{\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\langle\mathrm{D}F(x+t(y-x))(y-x),\mathrm{D}F(x+s(y-x))(y-x)\rangle\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}s}

(187)

\displaystyle\qquad\textstyle{=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}(y-x)^{\top}H(x_{s},x_{t})(y-x)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}s}

(188)

\displaystyle\qquad\textstyle{=(y-x)^{\top}H(x_{0}^{k},x_{0}^{k})(y-x)+\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}(y-x)^{\top}(H(x_{t},x_{s})-H(x_{0}^{k},x_{0}^{k}))(y-x)\mathrm{d}t\mathrm{d}s}

(189)

\displaystyle\qquad\geq(m/2)\left\|y-x\right\|^{2}\;,

(190)

where
x_{t}=x+t(y-x)
, which concludes the proof.
∎

A.3
Quantitative control of the derivative

Lemma A.5
.

Under the same assumptions as
Section

4.1.1
, there exist
\eta>0
and

P\in\mathtt{Poly}(4,\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that for
x\in F^{-1}(0)
and

t\in\operatorname{B}_{\infty}(0,\eta)
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{D}_{t}\chi(t,x)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq(1+M_{0,\varphi}+M_{1,\varphi})(1+M_{0,\Psi}+M_{1,\Psi})

(191)

\displaystyle\quad\times P(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})\exp[P(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})]\;,

(192)

where we recall that
\chi
is defined in (
83
) and for any

\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
i\in\{0,\dots,\ell\}
and

f\in\mathrm{C}^{\ell}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{p})
,

M_{i,f}=\sup\{\|\mathrm{D}^{i}f(x)\|\,:\;x\in F^{-1}(\operatorname{B}_{\infty}(0,\eta))\}
.

Proof.

In this proof, for any
f:\ \mathbb{R}^{m_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}^{m_{1}}
with

m_{0},m_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
differentiable, we denote
\mathrm{d}f
its differential.
Recall that
\bar{\Phi}_{t}:\ \mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined such that for
any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\bar{\Phi}_{t}(x)=x^{(p)}
with
x^{(0)}=x
and for
any
i\in\{0,\dots,p-1\}
,
x^{(i+1)}=\Phi_{i+1}(t_{i+1},x^{(i)})
,
with
\Phi_{i+1}
given by (
80
). The compact sets
\mathsf{K}_{0}
and

\mathsf{K}_{1}
are defined in the proof of
Section

4.1.1
. Since for any

x\in F^{-1}(0)
,
|\det(\mathrm{D}\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x))|>0
, we
assume without loss of generality that

\det(\mathrm{D}\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x))>0
. For ease of notation we denote

\varphi_{\Psi}=\varphi\times\Psi
. We have

M_{0,\varphi_{\Psi}}=M_{0,\varphi}M_{0,\Psi}\;,\qquad M_{1,\varphi_{\Psi}}=M_{0,\varphi}M_{1,\Psi}+M_{1,\varphi}M_{0,\Psi}\;.

(193)

In addition, we have for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
t\in\mathsf{K}_{0}

\chi(t,x)=\varphi_{\Psi}(\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x))\mathrm{J}F(\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x))^{-1}\det(\mathrm{D}\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}(x))\;.

(194)

We now control the first derivative of
\chi
. We divide the rest of the proof in three steps.

(a)

We start by providing upper bounds for
x\mapsto h_{i,j}(x)
,

x\mapsto\mathrm{D}h_{i,j}(x)
and
x\mapsto\mathrm{D}^{2}h_{i,j}(x)
for any

i,j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
where we recall that for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
we
have

\{h_{i,j}(x)\}_{1\leq i,j\leq p}=G(x)^{-1}=\mathrm{Adj}(G(x))/\det(G(x))=\mathrm{Adj}(G(x))/\mathrm{J}F(x)^{2}\;,

(195)

where
\mathrm{Adj}(G(x))
is the adjugate of

G(x)=\{\langle\nabla F_{i}(x),\nabla F_{j}(x)\rangle\}_{1\leq i,j\leq p}
, where
for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
and
i,j\in\{1,\dots,p\}

\mathrm{Adj}(G(x))=\{(-1)^{i+j}\det(G^{i,j}(x))\}_{1\leq i,j\leq p}\;,

(196)

where for any
i,j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
,
\det(G^{i,j}(x))
is the
(i,j)

minor of
G(x)
. Hence, using the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality there exists

D_{0}\geq 0
such that for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
and
i,j\in\{1,\dots,p\}

\left|h_{i,j}(x)\right|\leq D_{0}M_{1,F}^{2p-2}/m_{1,F}^{2}\;,

(197)

where we define

M_{1,F}=\sup\{\left\|\nabla F_{i}(x)\right\|\,:\;x\in\mathsf{K}_{1},\ i\in\{1,\dots,p\}\}\;,\quad m_{1,F}=\inf\{\mathrm{J}F(x)\,:\;x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}\}\;.

(198)

Recall that
m_{1,F}>0
since
\mathsf{K}_{1}
is compact and for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,
\mathrm{J}F(x)>0
.
We have that for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
i,j\in\{1,\dots,p\}

\mathrm{d}h_{i,j}(x)(u)=e_{i}^{\top}\mathrm{d}G(x)^{-1}(u)e_{j}=-e_{i}^{\top}G(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}G(x)(u)G(x)^{-1}e_{j}\;.

(199)

In addition, we have for any
x,u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
i,j\in\{1,\dots,p\}

\mathrm{d}G_{i,j}(x)(u)=\mathrm{d}^{2}F_{i}(x)(\nabla F_{j}(x),u)+\mathrm{d}^{2}F_{j}(x)(\nabla F_{i}(x),u)\;.

(200)

Combining this result and (
199
) we get that there exists
C_{1}\geq 0
such that for any

x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
and
i,j\in\{1,\dots,p\}

\left\|\mathrm{D}h_{i,j}(x)\right\|\leq C_{1}M_{1,F}^{4p-3}M_{2,F}/m_{1,F}^{4}\;,

(201)

where

M_{2,F}=\sup\{\|\nabla^{2}F_{i}(x)\|\,:\;x\in\mathsf{K}_{1},\ i\in\{1,\dots,p\}\}
. Hence using (
78
), (
197
) and
(
201
), there exist
C_{2},C_{3}\geq 0
such that for any

x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
and
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}

\left\|g_{i}(x)\right\|\leq C_{2}M_{1,F}^{2p-1}/m_{1,F}^{2}\;,\quad\left\|\mathrm{D}g_{i}(x)\right\|\leq C_{3}M_{2,F}/m_{1,F}^{2}\{M_{1,F}^{4p-2}/m_{1,F}^{2}+M_{1,F}^{2p-2}\}\;.

(202)

Similarly, for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,
u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and

i,j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{2}h_{i,j}(x)(u,v)

\displaystyle=e_{i}^{\top}\mathrm{d}^{2}G(x)^{-1}(u,v)e_{j}

(203)

\displaystyle=e_{i}^{\top}\left\{G(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}G(x)(u)G(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}G(x)(v)G(x)^{-1}\right.

(204)

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+G(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}G(x)(v)G(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}G(x)(u)G(x)^{-1}\right.

(205)

\displaystyle\qquad\left.-G(x)^{-1}\mathrm{d}^{2}G(x)(u,v)G(x)^{-1}\right\}e_{j}\;.

(206)

We also have for any
x,u,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
i,j\in\{1,\dots,p\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}^{2}G_{i,j}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{3}F_{i}(x)(\nabla F_{j}(x),u,v)+\mathrm{d}^{3}F_{j}(x)(\nabla F_{i}(x),u,v)

(207)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathrm{d}^{2}F_{i}(x)(\mathrm{d}^{2}F_{j}(x)(v),u)+\mathrm{d}^{2}F_{j}(x)(\mathrm{d}^{2}F_{i}(x)(v),u)\;.

(208)

Hence there exist
P_{3}\in\mathtt{Poly}(4,\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
we have

\|\mathrm{D}^{2}h_{i,j}(x)\|\leq P_{3}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})\;,

(209)

where

M_{3,F}=\sup\{\|\nabla^{3}F_{i}(x)\|\,:\;x\in\mathsf{K}_{1},\ i\in\{1,\dots,p\}\}
. Next, note that for any
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
,
we can choose
g_{i}
such that
g_{i}\in\mathrm{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
(and
therefore
\Phi_{i}\in\mathrm{C}^{3,2}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathbb{R}^{d})
by
Lemma

C.1
).
Combining this result, (
202
) and (
209
), there
exist
P_{1}\in\mathtt{Poly}(2,\mathbb{R}_{+})
,
P_{2}\in\mathtt{Poly}(3,\mathbb{R}_{+})
and

P_{3}\in\mathtt{Poly}(4,\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
and

i\in\{1,\dots,p\}

\displaystyle\left\|g_{i}(x)\right\|\leq P_{1}(M_{1,F},1/m_{1,F})\;,\qquad\left\|\mathrm{D}g_{i}(x)\right\|\leq P_{2}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},1/m_{1,F})\;,

(210)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{D}^{2}g_{i}(x)\|\leq P_{3}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})\;.

(211)

(b)

For any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such
that
\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
we have
\left|t_{i}\right|\leq 2\eta
, since

F_{i}(\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x))=F_{i}(x)-t_{i}
. Hence combining this result, the fact
that for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\Phi_{i}(0,x)=x
, (
80
) and
(
202
), for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and

t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
we have

\textstyle{\left\|\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\right\|\leq\left\|x\right\|+\int_{0}^{t_{i}}\left\|\mathrm{D}_{t}\Phi_{i}(s,x)\right\|\mathrm{d}s\leq\left\|x\right\|+2\eta C_{2}M_{1,F}^{2p-1}/m_{1,F}^{2}\;.}

(212)

Therefore, there exists
C_{4}\geq 0
such that for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,

i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
we have

\left\|\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\right\|\leq C_{4}(1+M_{1,F}^{2p-1}/m_{1,F}^{2})\;,\quad\left\|\mathrm{D}_{t}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\right\|\leq C_{2}M_{1,F}^{2p-1}/m_{1,F}^{2}\;.

(213)

Similarly, for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and

t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathrm{D}_{x}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\right\|

\displaystyle\textstyle{\leq\left\|\operatorname{Id}\right\|+\int_{0}^{t_{i}}\left\|\mathrm{D}_{t,x}\Phi_{i}(s,x)\right\|\left\|\mathrm{D}_{x}\Phi_{i}(s,x)\right\|\mathrm{d}s}

(214)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\leq\left\|\operatorname{Id}\right\|+\int_{0}^{t_{i}}\left\|\mathrm{D}g_{i}(\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x))\right\|\left\|\mathrm{D}_{x}\Phi_{i}(s,x)\right\|\mathrm{d}s}

(215)

\displaystyle\textstyle{\leq\left\|\operatorname{Id}\right\|+C_{3}(M_{2,F}/m_{1,F}^{2})\{M_{1,F}^{4p-2}/m_{1,F}^{2}+M_{1,F}^{2p-2}\}\int_{0}^{t_{i}}\left\|\mathrm{D}_{x}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\right\|\mathrm{d}s\;.}

(216)

Hence, using Grönwall’s lemma, for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,

i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
we have

\left\|\mathrm{D}_{x}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\right\|\leq\left\|\operatorname{Id}\right\|\exp[2\eta C_{3}(M_{2,F}/m_{1,F}^{2})\{M_{1,F}^{4p-2}/m_{1,F}^{2}+M_{1,F}^{2p-2}\}]\;.

(217)

Therefore, there exists
C_{5}\geq 0
such that for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,

i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
with
\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\in\mathsf{K}_{1}

we have

\left\|\mathrm{D}_{x}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\right\|\leq C_{5}\exp[C_{5}(M_{2,F}/m_{1,F}^{2})\{M_{1,F}^{4p-2}/m_{1,F}^{2}+M_{1,F}^{2p-2}\}]\;.

(218)

Using this result, (
80
) and (
210
), there
exists
C_{6}\geq 0
such that for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,

i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
with
\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\in\mathsf{K}_{1}

we have

\left\|\mathrm{D}_{t,x}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\right\|\leq C_{6}\exp[C_{6}(M_{2,F}/m_{1,F}^{2})\{M_{1,F}^{4p-2}/m_{1,F}^{2}+M_{1,F}^{2p-2}\}]\;.

(219)

Hence combining (
213
), (
218
) and
(
219
), there exist
P_{4}\in\mathtt{Poly}(2,\mathbb{R}_{+})
and

P_{5}\in\mathtt{Poly}(3,\mathbb{R}_{+})
such
that for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
,
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
with

\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
we have

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{D}_{t}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\|+\|\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\|\leq P_{4}(M_{1,F},1/m_{1,F})\;,

(220)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{D}_{x}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\|+\|\mathrm{D}_{t,x}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\|\leq P_{5}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},1/m_{1,F})\exp[P_{5}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},1/m_{1,F})]\;.

(221)

In addition, using (
80
) we have for
any
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
,
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\mathrm{D}_{t}\mathrm{D}_{x}^{2}\Phi_{i}(s,x)=\mathrm{D}g_{i}(\Phi_{i}(s,x))\mathrm{D}_{x}^{2}\Phi_{i}(s,x)+\mathrm{D}^{2}g_{i}(\Phi_{i}(s,x))\mathrm{D}_{x}\Phi_{i}(s,x)\;.

(222)

Therefore, using (
210
) and (
220
),
we have for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}

such that
\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\in\mathsf{K}_{1}

\displaystyle\textstyle{\|\mathrm{D}_{x}^{2}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\|\leq\int_{0}^{t_{i}}\{\|\mathrm{D}g_{i}(\Phi_{i}(s,x))\|\|\mathrm{D}_{x}^{2}\Phi_{i}(s,x)\|+\|\mathrm{D}^{2}g_{i}(\Phi_{i}(s,x))\|\|\mathrm{D}_{x}\Phi_{i}(s,x)\|\}\mathrm{d}s}

(223)

\displaystyle\leq 2\eta P_{3}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})P_{5}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},1/m_{1,F})\exp[P_{5}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},1/m_{1,F})]

(224)

\displaystyle\qquad\textstyle{+P_{2}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},1/m_{1,F})\int_{0}^{t_{i}}\|\mathrm{D}_{x}^{2}\Phi_{i}(s,x)\|\mathrm{d}s\;.}

(225)

Hence, using Grönwall’s lemma, there exists

P_{6}\in\mathtt{Poly}(4,\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that for any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,

i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
we have

\|\mathrm{D}_{x}^{2}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\|\leq P_{6}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})\exp[P_{6}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})]\;.

(226)

Hence, combining this result and (
220
), we have for
any
x\in\mathsf{K}_{1}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
and
t_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\in\mathsf{K}_{1}

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{D}_{t}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\|+\|\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\|\leq P_{4}(M_{1,F},1/m_{1,F})\;,

(227)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{D}_{x}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\|+\|\mathrm{D}_{t,x}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\|\leq P_{5}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},1/m_{1,F})\exp[P_{5}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},1/m_{1,F})]\;,

(228)

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{D}_{x}^{2}\Phi_{i}(t_{i},x)\|\leq P_{6}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})\exp[P_{6}(M_{1,F},M_{2,F},M_{3,F},1/m_{1,F})]\;.

(229)

(c)

In what follows, we fix
t\in\operatorname{B}_{\infty}(0,\eta)
and use
(
229
) to provide uniform bounds for

\mathrm{D}_{t}\bar{\Phi}^{-1}_{t}
,
\mathrm{D}_{t}\mathrm{D}\bar{\Phi}^{-1}_{t}
and

\mathrm{D}\bar{\Phi}^{-1}_{t}
on
F^{-1}(0)
. We introduce

\{\bar{\Phi}_{i,t}^{-1}\}_{i=1}^{p}
such that for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
and

i\in\{1,\dots,p\}

\bar{\Phi}_{t,i}^{-1}(x)=\Phi_{i}(-t_{i},\bar{\Phi}_{t,i+1}^{-1}(x))\;,\qquad\bar{\Phi}_{t,p+1}^{-1}(x)=x\;.

(230)

Note that
\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}=\bar{\Phi}_{t,1}^{-1}
. Let
j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
.
For any
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
we distinguish three cases:

(i)

i>j
, then for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
,
\mathrm{D}_{t_{j}}\bar{\Phi}_{t,i}^{-1}(x)=0
.

(ii)

i=j
, then for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
,
\mathrm{D}_{t_{j}}\bar{\Phi}_{t,i}^{-1}(x)=-\mathrm{D}_{t}\Phi_{j}(-t_{j},\bar{\Phi}_{t,j+1}^{-1}(x))
.

(iii)

i<j
, then for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
,

\mathrm{D}_{t_{j}}\bar{\Phi}_{t,i}^{-1}(x)=\mathrm{D}_{x}\Phi_{i}(-t_{i},\bar{\Phi}_{t,i+1}^{-1}(x))\mathrm{D}_{t_{j}}\bar{\Phi}_{t,i+1}^{-1}(x)
.

Combining these results, (
229
) and the fact that

\bar{\Phi}_{t}^{-1}=\bar{\Phi}_{t,1}^{-1}
we get that there exists

\bar{P}_{7}\in\mathtt{Poly}(3,\mathbb{R}_{+})
such that for any
x\in F^{-1}(0)
, and

j\in\{1,\dots,p\}
we have
